Homomorphisms into Specht modules labelled by hooks in quantum characteristic two
Berta Hudak

Abstract
. Let R n R_{n} denote the KLR algebra of type A e − 1 ( 1 ) A^{(1)}_{e-1} . Using the presentation of Specht modules given by Kleschev–Mathas–Ram, Loubert completely determined Hom R n ⁡ ( S μ , S λ ) \Hom_{R_{n}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda}) where μ \mu is an arbitrary partition, λ \lambda is a hook and e ≠ 2 e\neq 2 . In this paper, we investigate the same problem when e = 2 e=2 . First we give a complete description of the action of the generators on the basis elements of S λ S^{\lambda} . We use this result to identify a large family of partitions μ \mu such that there exists at least one non-zero homomorphism from S μ S^{\mu} to S λ S^{\lambda} , explicitly describe these maps and give their grading. Finally, we generalise James’s result for the trivial module.

†
†

2020 Mathematics subject classification: 20C08, 05E10

1.
Introduction

The
Iwahori–Hecke algebra

\mathcal{H}_{n}
of type
A
arises in various mathematical settings, and its representation theory is very similar to the modular representation theory of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.
Let
q
denote the deformation parameter of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
and set
e
to be the smallest integer such that
1+q+q^{2}+\dots+q^{e-1}=0
(if no such integer exists, we set
e=0
).
For every partition
\lambda
of
n
we can associate a corresponding
\mathcal{H}_{n}
-module, called a
Specht module

S^{\lambda}
.

Brundan and Kleshchev showed in
[
BK09
]

that over an arbitrary field
\mathbb{F}
, the algebra
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is isomorphic to a certain algebra
R^{\Lambda}_{n}
, known as the
cyclotomic Khovanov–Lauda–Rouquier
(KLR) algebra of type
A
.
Moreover,
R^{\Lambda}_{n}
is
\mathbb{Z}
-graded and this grading can be transferred to
\mathcal{H}_{n}
via the mentioned isomorphism.
In
[
BKW11
]
, Specht modules were described as graded modules over
R^{\Lambda}_{n}
and this result was extended in
[
KMR12
]
where the authors gave a construction of Specht modules in terms of generators and relations.

In this paper, we use this presentation to consider
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
where
\mu
is an arbitrary partition,
\lambda
is a hook and
e=2
.
The significantly simpler situation when the quantum characteristic is at least
3
was solved earlier by Loubert
[
Lou17
]
.
The defining relations of
R^{\Lambda}_{n}
are different when
e=2
, so it requires a completely separate treatment and our work turns out to be a lot more complicated than the
e\geq 3
case.
In particular, when
e\geq 3

\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is at most one-dimensional with any nonzero homomorphism sending the cyclic generator of
S^{\mu}
to a single standard basis vector in
S^{\lambda}
.
As we will later see, here this is different: dimensions of the homomorphism space quickly grow very large and each map involves an integer linear combination of standard basis vectors.
Nevertheless, in the case when
\mu
is a
two-column hook partition
, a partition of the form
(c,2^{s},1^{d})
, we completely determine when
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is nontrivial if
c
is even or
s=0
and construct at least one homomorphism explicitly.

Main Theorem

Let
\lambda=(a,1^{b})
and
\mu=(c,2^{s},1^{d})
where if
s>0
then
c
is even. Then
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is at least one dimensional if for
c
even, we have that
a\geq s+2
; and

•

n
is odd,
n+a\equiv_{2}s+1
and
s+1\leq a\leq n-(s+1)
, or

•

n
is even and
s+2\leq a\leq c+s
or
a=c+s+1+2y
for
0\leq y\leq d/2-2
,

and zero otherwise.

(The above theorem appears as
4.36
later.)

In the special cases when
\lambda
is a single row or column, our result allows us to determine whether
S^{\mu}
has the trivial module as a quotient.
Furthermore, by the following isomorphism, it also enables us to immediately see whether there exists a non-trivial homomorphism in the other direction (see
[
Lou17
]
for more details).
Let
\lambda^{\prime}
denote the
conjugate
of
\lambda
, which is the partition obtained from
\lambda
by swapping its rows and columns.
Then we have that

\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\lambda^{\prime}},S^{\mu^{\prime}}).

By the above isomorphism, it is easy to apply
4.36
to determine whether
S^{\mu}
has a one-dimensional submodule.

Structure of the paper
In
section

2
, we set up notation and collect all necessary background material.
In particular, we introduce cyclotomic KLR algebras of type
A
and the universal Specht modules, following
[
KMR12
]
.
In
section

3
, we completely determine the action of the generator elements on the basis elements
v_{\mathtt{T}}
of
S^{\lambda}
.
Our findings (see
3.30
) generalise the results in
[
Spe14
]
and
[
Spe15
, Chapter 3.]
for all standard tableaux of hook shapes and all parities of
n
.
In
section

4
, we first discover a novel way to label hook tableaux by partitions and a rule to assign a coefficient to each one of them.
This leads us to completely determine the homomorphisms from
S^{\mu}
to
S^{\lambda}
where
\mu
is a two-column hook partition, that is a partition of the form
(c,2^{s},1^{d})
if
c
is even or
s=0
(see
4.36
).
Finally, we generalise James’s well-known result for the trivial module.
Using a different technique inspired by
[
DG15
]
, in the special case when
\mu
is a two-part partition and
n
is odd, we are able to prove when the homomorphism space
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is non-trivial without explicitly describing the maps.

ArXiv version

We decided to omit some of the more lengthy but less informative calculations from this pdf as they are similar to others included.
However for those interested, we suggest downloading the
L
a
T
e
X
source and find the toggle near the beginning of the file which allows one to compile the paper with these details included.

The author thanks Dr Liron Speyer for his helpful comments and inspiring correspondence.
She would also like to thank Dr Haralampos Geranios for pointing out the argument given in
4.40
after most of the work in this paper has been completed.

2.
Background

For
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
, we write
m\equiv_{2}m^{\prime}
if
m-m^{\prime}
is even and
m\not\equiv_{2}m^{\prime}
otherwise.

2.1.
Lie theoretical notation

Take
e=2
and
I:=\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}=\{0,1\}
.
Let
\Gamma
be the quiver of type
A_{e-1}^{(1)}=A_{1}^{(1)}
with vertex set
I
as depicted below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\qquad 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

The corresponding
Cartan matrix

\mathsf{A}=(a_{i,j})_{i,j\in I}
is given by

\begin{pmatrix}2&-2\\
-2&2\end{pmatrix}.

Let
(\mathsf{A},P,\Pi,\Pi^{\vee})
be a realization of the Cartan matrix
(a_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
, so we have the
simple roots

\Pi=\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\},
and
the
fundamental dominant weights

\{\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1}\}
in the
weight lattice

P
.
Let
Q^{+}:=\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\alpha_{i}
be the
positive cone of the root lattice
.

For
\alpha=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\alpha_{i}\in Q^{+}
, let
{\operatorname{ht}}(\alpha)
denote the
height of

\alpha
, that is
{\operatorname{ht}}(\alpha)=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
denote the
symmetric group
on
n
letters and let
s_{r}=(r,r+1)
, for
1\leq r<n
, be the simple transpositions of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. Then
\mathfrak{S}_{n}

acts from the left on the set
I^{n}
by place permutations. If
\text{\boldmath$i$}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})\in I^{n}

then its
weight
is
|\text{\boldmath$i$}|:=\alpha_{i_{1}}+\cdots+\alpha_{i_{n}}\in Q^{+}
. Then the

\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-orbits on
I^{n}
are the sets

I^{\alpha}:=\{\text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{n}\mid\alpha=|\text{\boldmath$i$}|\}

parameterised by all
\alpha\in Q^{+}
of height
n
.

2.2.
Partitions and tableaux

Let
{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
be the set of all partitions of
n
and fix
{\mathcal{P}}:=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 0}{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
.
The
Young diagram
of the partition
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}
is defined as

[\mu]:=\{(r,c)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\mid 1\leq c\leq\mu_{r}\}.

We call the elements of this set the
nodes of
\mu
.
To each node
A=(r,c)\in[\mu]

we associate its
residue
given by:

\res A=(c-r)\;\text{ }(\text{\rm mod }2)\,.

An
i
-node
is a node of residue
i
.
Define the
residue content of
\mu
to be

\operatorname{cont}(\mu):=\sum_{A\in\mu}\alpha_{\res A}\in Q^{+}.

A node
A\in[\mu]
is a
removable node
(of
\mu
), if
[\mu]\setminus\{A\}
is (the diagram of) a partition. A node
B\not\in[\mu]
is an
addable node
(of
\mu
), if
[\mu]\cup\{B\}
is a partition.
We use the notation

\mu_{A}:=[\mu]\setminus\{A\}.

If
\mu,\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
, we say that
\mu

dominates

\nu
, and write
\mu\unrhd\nu
, if

\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mu_{j}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{i}\nu_{j}

for all
i\geq 1
.
In other words,
\mu
dominates
\nu
if
[\mu]
can be obtained from
[\nu]
by moving nodes up in the diagram.

If
\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k})\in{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
, then the
conjugate
of
\mu
is the partition

\mu^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
where
\mu^{\prime}
is obtained by swapping the rows and columns of
\mu
.

Let
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
.
A
\mu
-tableau

\mathtt{T}
is obtained from
[\mu]
by
inserting the integers
1,\dots,n
into the nodes without any repeats.
If the node
A=(r,c)\in[\mu]
is occupied by the integer
m
in
\mathtt{T}
, we write
m=\mathtt{T}(r,c)
and set

\res_{\mathtt{T}}(m)=\res A
. The
residue sequence
of
\mathtt{T}
is

\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})\in I^{n},

where
i_{m}=\res_{\mathtt{T}}(m)
is the residue of the node occupied by

m
in
\mathtt{T}
(
1\leq m\leq n
).

A
\mu
-tableau
\mathtt{T}
is
row-standard
(resp.
column-standard
) if its entries increase from left to right (resp. from top to bottom) along the rows (resp. columns) of each component of
\mathtt{T}
.
A
\mu
-tableau
\mathtt{T}
is
standard
if it is both row- and column-standard.
Let
\operatorname{Std}(\mu)
be the set of standard
\mu
-tableaux.

For
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}
,
i\in I
, and a removable
i
-node
A\in[\mu]
, we define

d_{A}(\mu)=\#\Big\{\vbox{\centering addable $i$-nodes of\\
$\mu$ strictly below $A$\@add@centering}\Big\}-\#\Big\{\vbox{\centering removable $i$-nodes of\\
$\mu$ strictly below $A$\@add@centering}\Big\}.

Given
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
and
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\mu)
, the
degree
of
\mathtt{T}
is defined inductively. If
n=0
, then
\mathtt{T}=\emptyset
, and set
\deg(\mathtt{T}):=0
.
Otherwise, let
A
be the node occupied by
n
in
\mathtt{T}
. Let
\mathtt{T}_{<n}\in\operatorname{Std}(\mu_{A})

be the tableau obtained from
\mathtt{T}
by removing this node and set

\deg(\mathtt{T}):=d_{A}(\mu)+\deg(\mathtt{T}_{<n}).

The symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
acts on the set of
\mu
-tableaux from the left by acting on the entries of the tableaux.
Let
\mathtt{T}^{\mu}
be the unique
\mu
-tableau in which the numbers
1,2,\dots,n
appear in order from left to right along the successive rows,
working from top to bottom.
We call
\mathtt{T}^{\mu}
the
(row) initial tableau
.

Set

\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}:=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}^{\mu}}.

For each
\mu
-tableau
\mathtt{T}
define permutations
w^{\mathtt{T}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
by the equation

w^{\mathtt{T}}\mathtt{T}^{\mu}=\mathtt{T}.

2.3.
Bruhat order

Let
\ell
be the length function on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
with respect to the Coxeter generators

s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{n-1}
. Let
\leq
be the
Bruhat order
on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
(so that
1\leq w
for all

w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n})
.
Define a related
partial order on
\operatorname{Std}(\mu)
as follows: if
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\mu)
then

\mathtt{T}\ledom\mathtt{S}\quad\text{if and only if}\quad w^{\mathtt{S}}\leq w^{\mathtt{T}}.

If
\mathtt{S}\ledom\mathtt{T}
and
\mathtt{S}\neq\mathtt{T}
, we write
\mathtt{S}\ldom\mathtt{T}
.
Observe that if
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\mu)
then
\mathtt{T}\ledom\mathtt{T}^{\mu}
.

2.4.
KLR algebras

We mostly follow the notations and conventions of
[
KMR12
]
.
Let
\mathbb{F}
be a field and fix
\alpha\in Q^{+}
such that
{\operatorname{ht}}(\alpha)=n
.
We define the algebra
R_{\alpha}
to be the unital associative
\mathbb{F}
-algebra generated by elements

\{e(\text{\boldmath$i$})\>|\>\text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{\alpha}\}\cup\{y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\}\cup\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n-1}\},

subject only to the following relations:

\displaystyle e(\text{\boldmath$i$})e(\text{\boldmath$j$})

\displaystyle=\delta_{\text{\boldmath$i$},\text{\boldmath$j$}}e(\text{\boldmath$i$});

\displaystyle\sum_{\text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{\alpha}}e(\text{\boldmath$i$})

\displaystyle=1;

\displaystyle y_{r}e(\text{\boldmath$i$})

\displaystyle=e(\text{\boldmath$i$})y_{r};

\displaystyle\psi_{r}e(\text{\boldmath$i$})

\displaystyle=e(s_{r}{}\text{\boldmath$i$})\psi_{r};

\displaystyle y_{r}y_{s}

\displaystyle=y_{s}y_{r};

\displaystyle\psi_{r}y_{s}

\displaystyle=y_{s}\psi_{r}\hskip 142.26378pt\text{if $s\neq r,r+1$};

\displaystyle\psi_{r}\psi_{s}

\displaystyle=\psi_{s}\psi_{r}\hskip 139.41832pt\text{if }r\neq s\pm 1;

\displaystyle\psi_{r}y_{r+1}e(\text{\boldmath$i$})

\displaystyle=(y_{r}\psi_{r}+\delta_{i_{r},i_{r+1}})e(\text{\boldmath$i$})

\displaystyle y_{r+1}\psi_{r}e(\text{\boldmath$i$})

\displaystyle=(\psi_{r}y_{r}+\delta_{i_{r},i_{r+1}})e(\text{\boldmath$i$})

\displaystyle\psi_{r}^{2}e(\text{\boldmath$i$})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\quad\text{if }i_{r}=i_{r+1},\\
(y_{r+1}-y_{r})(y_{r}-y_{r+1})e(\text{\boldmath$i$})&\quad\text{if }i_{r}\neq i_{r+1};\end{cases}

\displaystyle(\psi_{r}\psi_{r+1}\psi_{r}-\psi_{r+1}\psi_{r}\psi_{r+1})e(\text{\boldmath$i$})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(y_{r}-2y_{r+1}+y_{r+2}\big)e(\text{\boldmath$i$})&\qquad\;\text{if }i_{r+2}=i_{r}\neq i_{r+1},\\
e(\text{\boldmath$i$})&\qquad\;\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

for all admissible
\text{\boldmath$i$},\text{\boldmath$j$},r,s
.

We define the
affine Khovanov–Lauda–Rouquier (KLR) algebra

R_{n}
of type
A^{(1)}_{1}
to be the direct sum

R_{n}:=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in Q^{+}\\
{\operatorname{ht}}(\alpha)=n\end{subarray}}R_{\alpha}

Fix
\Lambda=\Lambda_{0}\in P^{+}
.
The corresponding
cyclotomic KLR algebra (of type
A
)

R_{n}^{\Lambda}
is defined to be the quotient of
R_{n}
subject to the additional
cyclotomic relations
(of level one)

(2.1)

y_{1}=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad e(\text{\boldmath$i$})=0\text{ if }i_{1}\neq 0.

The algebras
R_{n}
and
R_{n}^{\Lambda}
are non-trivially
\mathbb{Z}
-graded, by setting

e(\text{\boldmath$i$})
to be of degree 0,

y_{r}
of degree
2
, and

\psi_{r}e(\text{\boldmath$i$})
of degree
-a_{i_{r},i_{r+1}}

for all
r
and
\text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{\alpha}
.

For the rest of this paper, we fix a
preferred reduced expression

w=s_{r_{1}}\dots s_{r_{m}}
for each
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
(
1\leq r_{1},\dots,r_{m}<n
) and we define the elements
\psi_{w}:=\psi_{r_{1}}\dots\psi_{r_{m}}\in R_{n}
.

Furthermore, for
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
and some tableau
\mathtt{T}
of shape
\mu
, we set
\psi^{\mathtt{T}}:=\psi_{w^{\mathtt{T}}}
.

2.5.
Universal Specht modules

Fix a partition
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
.
A node
A=(r,c)\in\mu
is called a
Garnir node
of
\mu
if we also have
(r+1,c)\in\mu
.
We define the
Garnir belt
of
A
to be the set
\mathbf{B}^{A}
of nodes of
\mu
containing
A
and all nodes directly to the right of
A
, along with the node directly below
A
and all nodes directly to the left of this node:

\mathbf{B}^{A}=\{(r,s)\in\mu\mid c\leq s\leq\mu_{r}\}\cup\{(r+1,s)\in\mu\mid 1\leq s\leq c\}.

For example, if
A=(2,3)
and
\mu=(6,5,3,2)
then
\mathbf{B}^{A}
consists of the highlighted nodes:

\begin{array}[]{l}\hbox to86.76pt{\vbox to57.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-6.11319pt\lower-50.19232pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}

We define an
A
-brick
to be a set of
e
successive nodes in the same row

\{(t,s),(t,s+1),\dots,(t,s+e-1)\}\subseteq\mathbf{B}^{A}

such that
\res(t,s)=\res(A)
.
Let
k\geq 0
be the number of bricks in
\mathbf{B}^{A}
.
We label the bricks

B^{A}_{1},B^{A}_{2},\dots,B^{A}_{k}

going from left to right along row
r
, and then from left to right along row
r+1
.
Define
C^{A}
to be the set of nodes in row
r
of
\mathbf{B}^{A}
not contained in any
A
-brick, and
D^{A}
to be the set of nodes in row
r+1
of
\mathbf{B}^{A}
not contained in any
A
-brick.
We see that as
e=2
, each brick consists of two nodes and
C^{A}
and
D^{A}
each contain at most one node.

Continuing with the previous example, taking
\mu=(6,5,3,2)
and
A=(2,3)
:

{}{{}}{}

Let
u,u+1,\dots,v
be the values in
\mathbf{B}^{A}
in the initial tableau
\mathtt{T}^{\mu}
.
We obtain a new tableau
\mathtt{T}^{A}
by placing the numbers
u,u+1\dots,v
from left to right in the following order:
D^{A}
,
B^{A}_{1}
,
B^{A}_{2}
, …,
B^{A}_{k}
,
C^{A}
.
The rest of the numbers are placed in the same positions as in
T^{\mu}
.
We also define
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{A}:=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}^{A}}
.

If
\mu
and
A
are the same as before, then we have that

\mathtt{T}^{A}=\begin{array}[]{l}\hbox to138.19pt{\vbox to89.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 33.518pt\lower-58.08147pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}

Assume that
k>0
, and let
l=\mathtt{T}^{A}(A)
and for
1\leq r<k
define

w^{A}_{r}:=\prod_{s=l+re-e}^{l+re-1}(s,s+e)\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}.

Informally,
w^{A}_{r}
swaps the bricks
B^{A}_{r}
and
B^{A}_{r+1}
. The elements
w^{A}_{1},w^{A}_{2},\dots,w^{A}_{k-1}
are Coxeter generators of the group

\mathfrak{S}^{A}:=\langle w^{A}_{1},w^{A}_{2},\dots,w^{A}_{k-1}\rangle\cong\mathfrak{S}_{k}.

By convention, if
k=0
we set
\mathfrak{S}^{A}
to be the trivial group.
Let
f
be the number of
A
-bricks in row
r
of
\mathbf{B}^{A}
.

We denote by
\mathscr{D}^{A}
the set of minimal length left coset representatives of
\mathfrak{S}_{f}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k-f}
in
\mathfrak{S}^{A}\cong\mathfrak{S}_{k}
.
The corresponding elements of
R^{\Lambda}_{n}
are given by

\sigma^{A}_{r}:=\psi_{w^{A}_{r}}e(\text{\boldmath$i$}^{A})\quad\text{and}\quad\tau^{A}_{r}:=(\sigma^{A}_{r}+1)e(\text{\boldmath$i$}^{A}).

Let
u\in\mathscr{D}^{A}
with reduced expression
u=w^{A}_{r_{1}}\dots w^{A}_{r_{a}}
.
Since every element of
\mathscr{D}^{A}
is fully commutative, the element
\tau^{A}_{u}:=\tau^{A}_{r_{1}}\dots\tau^{A}_{r_{a}}
does not depend upon this reduced expression.
Finally, we define the
Garnir element
to be

g^{A}:=\sum_{u\in\mathscr{D}^{A}}\tau^{A}_{u}\psi^{\mathtt{T}^{A}}\in R^{\Lambda}_{n}.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\alpha\in Q_{+}
,
n={\operatorname{ht}}(\alpha)
and
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
.
The
universal graded (row) Specht module

S^{\mu}
is defined to be the graded
R^{\Lambda}_{n}
-module generated by the vector
z^{\mu}
of degree
\deg(\mathtt{T}^{\mu})
subject only to the following relations:

(i)

e(\text{\boldmath$j$})z^{\mu}=\delta_{\text{\boldmath$j$},\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}}z^{\mu}
for all
\text{\boldmath$j$}\in I^{n}
;

(ii)

y_{r}z^{\mu}=0
for all
1\leq r\leq n
;

(iii)

\psi_{r}z^{\mu}=0
for all
1\leq r<n
such that
r
and
r+1
appear in the same row of
\mathtt{T}^{\mu}
;

(iv)

(homogeneous Garnir relations)
g^{A}z^{\mu}=0
for all Garnir nodes
A\in[\mu]
.

For each
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\mu)
, we define
v_{\mathtt{T}}:=\psi_{w^{\mathtt{T}}}z^{\mu}\in S^{\mu}
with residue sequence
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}:=w^{\mathtt{T}}\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}
.

Theorem 2.2

[
KMR12
, Corollary 6.24]
.

Let
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
. Then the universal Specht module
S^{\mu}
for
R^{\Lambda}_{n}
has homogeneous
\mathbb{F}
-basis

\{v_{\mathtt{T}}\mid\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\mu)\}.

3.
Specht modules indexed by hook partitions

In this section, we give a complete description of the action of the generators on the basis elements of the Specht module
S^{\lambda}
.

For the rest of this paper, we fix
\lambda:=(a,1^{b})
such that
a>0,b\geq 0
and
n=a+b
.

Definition 3.1
.

Given
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
, we call the nodes in positions
(1,y)
of
\lambda
for
y=2,3,\dots,a

arm nodes
and we define
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})=\{w^{\mathtt{T}}(2),\dots,w^{\mathtt{T}}(a)\}
.
Similarly, we call the nodes in positions
(x,1)
of
\lambda
for
x=2,3,\dots,b

leg nodes
and
\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})=\{w^{\mathtt{T}}(a+1),\dots,w^{\mathtt{T}}(b)\}
.

Definition 3.2
.

For any odd
3\leq u\leq n
, we call
u-1
and
u
a
pair
of
\mathtt{T}
, denoted
\mathtt{p}_{u}
, whenever
u-1
appears directly in front of
u
in
\mathtt{T}
, i.e. if
u-1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
(resp.,
u-1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
) we also have that
u\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
(resp.,
u\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
).
We denote by
\text{\boldmath$i$}_{\mathtt{p}_{u}}:=(i_{u-1},i_{u})
the residue of the pair.

Remark
.

The previous definition and many of the following lemmas are very similar to those in
[
Spe14
, §5]
and
[
Spe15
, Chapter 3]
.
In particular, if both
n
and
a
are odd, our results agree.
However, in our case the residues of the pairs might be different.

Lemma 3.3

[
BKW11
, Lemma 4.4]
.

Then for any
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
, we have that
e(\text{\boldmath$i$})v_{\mathtt{T}}=\delta_{\text{\boldmath$i$},\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}}v_{\mathtt{T}}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
and write
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})
. For
1\leq r\leq n
, we have that

y_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\begin{cases}-v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}&\text{ if $i_{r}=i_{r+1}$, $r\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})$, and $r+1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})$},\\
v_{s_{r-1}\mathtt{T}}&\text{ if $i_{r-1}=i_{r}$, $r-1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})$, and $r\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})$},\\
0,&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

We set
\mathscr{D}^{\lambda}(\text{\boldmath$i$})=\{w^{\mathtt{T}}|\ \mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)\text{ and }\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}=\text{\boldmath$i$}\}
define the subgroup of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}

H(\text{\boldmath$i$}):=\langle s_{m}\mid s_{m}\text{\boldmath$i$}=\text{\boldmath$i$}\rangle
for
(m\geq 2)
.
Fix
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and let
\mathtt{S}
be the most dominant standard
\lambda
-tableau with residue sequence
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}
such that
\mathtt{S}=x\mathtt{T}^{\lambda}
.
By
[
Lou17
, Proposition 2.13]
, there exists a unique minimal length permutation
a\in\mathscr{D}^{\lambda}(\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}})
such that
w^{\mathtt{T}}=ua
for some
u\in H(\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}})
.
Given a reduced expression
u=s_{r_{1}}\dots s_{r_{a}}
, we see that
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi_{r_{1}}\dots\psi_{r_{a}}v_{\mathtt{S}}
.
Furthermore,
\deg(v_{\mathtt{S}})-\deg(v_{\mathtt{T}})=2a
, and so
v_{\mathtt{S}}
is the unique largest degree vector with residue sequence
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}
. In particular,
y_{s}v_{\mathtt{S}}=0
for
1\leq s\leq n
.
As
H(\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}})
is commutative, at most one of
r-1
and
r
is an element of
\{r_{1},\dots,r_{a}\}
.

First, assume that
r\in\{r_{1},\dots,r_{a}\}
, that is we have
r\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
and
r+1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
with
i_{r}=i_{r+1}
. As each
s_{r_{i}}
involved in
w
commutes with each other, we can assume that
r=r_{1}
. Then we have that

\displaystyle y_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}

\displaystyle=y_{r}\psi_{r}\psi_{r_{2}}\dots\psi_{r_{a}}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=(\psi_{r}y_{r+1}-1)\psi_{r_{2}}\dots\psi_{r_{a}}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{r}\psi_{r_{2}}\dots\psi_{r_{a}}y_{r+1}v_{\mathtt{S}}-\psi_{r_{2}}\dots\psi_{r_{a}}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=-\psi_{r_{2}}\dots\psi_{r_{a}}v_{\mathtt{S}}=-v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}.

Similarly, taking
r-1\in\{r_{1},\dots,r_{a}\}
is the same as saying that
r-1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
, and
r\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
with
i_{r-1}=i_{r}
. Suppose
r-1=r_{1}
, then we can compute:

\displaystyle y_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}

\displaystyle=y_{r}\psi_{r-1}\psi_{r_{2}}\dots\psi_{r_{a}}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=(\psi_{r-1}y_{r-1}+1)\psi_{r_{2}}\dots\psi_{r_{a}}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{r-1}\psi_{r_{2}}\dots\psi_{r_{a}}y_{r-1}v_{\mathtt{S}}+\psi_{r_{2}}\dots\psi_{r_{a}}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{r_{2}}\dots\psi_{r_{a}}v_{\mathtt{S}}=v_{s_{r-1}\mathtt{T}}.

If
r,r-1\notin\{r_{1},\dots,r_{a}\}
, then
y_{r}
commutes with each
\psi_{r_{i}}
, and hence
y_{r}\psi_{r_{1}}\dots\psi_{r_{a}}v_{\mathtt{S}}=\psi_{r_{1}}\dots\psi_{r_{a}}y_{r}v_{\mathtt{S}}=0
.
∎

Remark
.

Observe that due to the imposed residue conditions, for
r
even
y_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
if and only if
r\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
and
r+1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
.
If
r\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
r-1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
, we would have
r-3
entries to place in front of them in
\mathtt{T}
, where the number of entries in front of
r
and
r-1
needs to have the same parity in order to satisfy the condition
i_{r-1}=i_{r}
.
But as
r-3
is odd, we see that this is impossible.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
and
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})
.
We have that
\psi_{1}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
and if
r\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
r+1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
, then
\psi_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}
for
2\leq r\leq n-1
.

Proof.

We see that
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}
begins with
(0,1,\dots)
.
By the cyclotomic relations (see
section

2.4
),
\psi_{1}v_{\mathtt{T}}=e(s_{1}\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}})\psi_{1}v_{\mathtt{T}}
.
Hence, by looking at the residues we must have
\psi_{1}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
.

The second assertion is obvious.
∎

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
and
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})
. If
r-1,r\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
, and
r+1,r+2\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
, then

\psi_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\begin{cases}-2v_{s_{r-1}s_{r+1}s_{r}\mathtt{T}}&\text{ if $i_{r-1}=i_{r+1}$ and $i_{r}=i_{r+2}$},\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Assume that
r-1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
, and
r+1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
, so we have that
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi_{r}v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}
.
Thus
\psi_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi_{r}^{2}v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}
.
If
i_{r}=i_{r+1}
, then
\psi_{r}^{2}v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}=0
.

If
i_{r}\neq i_{r+1}
, then
\psi_{r}^{2}v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}=(y_{r+1}-y_{r})(y_{r}-y_{r+1})v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}=(2y_{r+1}y_{r}-y_{r}^{2}-y_{r+1}^{2})v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}
.
We consider each of the three terms separately.

As
r\in\operatorname{Arm}(s_{r}\mathtt{T})
,
3.4
shows that if
y_{r}v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
then
r-1\in\operatorname{Leg}(s_{r}\mathtt{T})
and
i_{r-1}=i_{r+1}
.
In this case,
y_{r}v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}=v_{s_{r-1}s_{r}\mathtt{T}}
.
Applying
y_{r}
once again, we see that
y_{r}v_{s_{r-1}s_{r}\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
if and only if
r+1\in\operatorname{Arm}(s_{r-1}s_{r}\mathtt{T})
.
But if
r+1
is in
\operatorname{Arm}(s_{r-1}s_{r}\mathtt{T})
, we must have that
r+1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
, a contradiction.
Hence
y_{r}^{2}v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}=0
.

We deal with the term
y_{r+1}^{2}v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}
in a similar manner.
As
r+1\in\operatorname{Leg}(s_{r}\mathtt{T})
, if
y_{r+1}v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
then
r+2\in\operatorname{Arm}(s_{r}\mathtt{T})
and
i_{r+1}\neq i_{r+2}
.
Then
y_{r+1}v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}=-v_{s_{r+1}s_{r}\mathtt{T}}
.
Applying
y_{r+1}
once again, we see that
y_{r+1}v_{s_{r+1}s_{r}\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
if and only if
r\in\operatorname{Leg}(s_{r+1}s_{r}\mathtt{T})
. But if
r\in\operatorname{Leg}(s_{r+1}s_{r}\mathtt{T})
, then we must have that
r\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
, which contradicts our original assumption.
Thus
y_{r+1}^{2}v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}=0
.

Finally, we look at the cross term.
If
y_{r+1}y_{r}v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
, then we have that
y_{r}v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
if and only if
r-1\in\operatorname{Leg}(s_{r}\mathtt{T})
and
i_{r-1}=i_{r+1}
. In this case,
y_{r}v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}=v_{s_{r-1}s_{r}\mathtt{T}}
.
We also see that
y_{r+1}v_{s_{r-1}s_{r}\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
if and only if
r+2\in\operatorname{Arm}(s_{r-1}s_{r}\mathtt{T})
and
i_{r}=i_{r+2}
. But
r+2\in\operatorname{Arm}(s_{r-1}s_{r}\mathtt{T})
if and only if
r+2\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and we conclude that
y_{r+1}y_{r}v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}=-v_{s_{r-1}s_{r+1}s_{r}\mathtt{T}}
.
∎

Remark
.

Note that if an even
r
is as in the previous statement, then
\psi_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
.
To see this, observe that due to the residue conditions
i_{r-1}=i_{r+1},i_{r}=i_{r+2}
, the number of entries in front of
r-1
and
r+1
in
\mathtt{T}
must have the same parity.
In total, we have
r-3
entries to divide between the arm and the leg of
\mathtt{T}
.
If
r
is even, then
r-3
is odd, hence it would be impossible to have both odd or even number of entries in front of
r-1
and
r+1
.

Definition 3.7

(
[
Spe14
]
, Definition 5.1)
.

We define
\Psi_{r}:=\psi_{r}\psi_{r+1}\psi_{r-1}\psi_{r}
.
For two odd integeres
3\leq x\leq y\leq n-2
, we set:

\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{x}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y}}{\downarrow}}:=\Psi_{y}\Psi_{y-2}\dots\Psi_{x}\quad\text{and}\quad\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{x}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y}}{\uparrow}}:=\Psi_{x}\Psi_{x+2}\dots\Psi_{y}.

If
y<x
, we consider both of the above terms to be the identity.

We will now describe how to write
v_{\mathtt{T}}
as a product of
\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{x}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y}}{\downarrow}}
’s.
Let
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
be a tableau in which all entries appear in pairs as in
3.2
.
Let
d
be the number of pairs in the arm of
\mathtt{T}
which differ from the corresponding pairs in
\mathtt{T}^{\lambda}
, we will label these by
y_{1}^{\prime},\dots,y_{d}^{\prime}
where
i_{j_{i}^{\prime}}=1
and define

j_{i}:=\begin{cases}j_{i}^{\prime}-1&\text{if $j^{\prime}_{i}$ is even}\\
j_{i}^{\prime}-2&\text{if $j^{\prime}_{i}$ is odd.}\end{cases}

This way we will always put in the highest possible pair into a given position.
Since
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
, we see that these are precisely the final
d
pairs in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
.

.

\lambda=(7,1^{6})
and
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
is as below

then
d=2
and we see that
j_{1}=7
and
j_{2}=11
.

Observe that
v_{\mathtt{T}}
has the reduced expression

v_{\mathtt{T}}=\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{a-2d+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{j_{1}}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{a-2d+4}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{j_{2}}}{\downarrow}}\dots\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{a-2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{j_{d-1}}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{a}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{j_{d}}}{\downarrow}}z^{\lambda}.

We will refer to this as the
standard form
for
v_{\mathtt{T}}
.
Notice that
j_{i+1}>j_{i}
for all
i=1,\dots,d-1
.
Moreover, if
v_{\mathtt{T}}\in S^{\lambda}
is in standard form, then any expression obtained from it by deleting
\Psi
terms from the left is also in
S^{\lambda}
.

Lemma 3.9

[
Spe14
, Lemma 5.3]
.

Let
\lambda
and
\mathtt{T}
be as above. Then

(1)

e(\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}})\Psi_{r}=\Psi_{r}e(\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}})
for all
r
;

(2)

y_{k}\Psi_{r}=\Psi_{r}y_{k}
for all
k\geq r+3
and for all
k\leq r-2
;

(3)

\psi_{k}\Psi_{r}=\Psi_{r}\psi_{k}
for all
k\geq r+3
and for all
k\leq r-3
.

If both
n
and
a
are odd and all entries appear in pairs in
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
, then
[
Spe15
, Proposition 3.27]
gives a complete description of the action of
\psi_{r}
for all
v_{\mathtt{T}}
and
2\leq r\leq n-1
.
Now, we will extend these results for all
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
on a case by case approach.
Set
\ell:=\mathtt{T}(1,a)
.

First, we will illustrate how to obtain
v_{\mathtt{T}}
for all
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
.

(1)

Move
\ell
into position
\mathtt{T}(1,a)
using
\psi_{\ell-1}\psi_{\ell-2}\dots\psi_{a+1}\psi_{a}
where
a<\ell\leq n-1
(this part only appears if
a
is even).

(2)

Move the pairs into position using some
\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{y}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{x}}{\downarrow}}
terms:
\mathtt{p}_{y}\mapsto\mathtt{p}_{x+2}
where
x
is maximal such that
x+1\leq\mathtt{T}(1,y-1)
and
x+2\leq\mathtt{T}(1,y)
.

(3)

Using some individual
\psi
’s, we either

•

“break up” the pairs (i.e. an even entry
j-1
will not have
j
next to it), or

•

shift the residues of a pair (i.e. for some
\mathtt{p}_{j}
we will have that
\text{\boldmath$i$}_{\mathtt{p}_{j}}=(0,1)
instead of
(1,0)
).

j_{1}=5,j_{2}=11,j_{3}=13
, hence

\mathtt{T}_{2}=\begin{array}[]{l}\hbox to144.71pt{\vbox to114.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-5.89336pt\lower-107.09784pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}

{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 -137.8 M 9.84 -147.64 M 9.84 -147.64 L 9.84 -127.95 L 29.53 -127.95 L 29.53 -147.64 Z M 29.53 -127.95}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 -137.8}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.22636pt}{-102.80687pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array},

v_{\mathtt{T}}=\underbrace{\psi_{11}\psi_{10}\psi_{9}\psi_{8}\psi_{7}}_{\scriptsize\text{``breaks" up $\mathtt{p}_{7}$}}\underbrace{\psi_{12}\psi_{13}\psi_{14}\psi_{15}}_{\scriptsize\text{puts $13,14,15,16$ in front of $17$}}\Psi_{5}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{7}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{11}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{9}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{13}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{16}\psi_{15}\dots\psi_{10}z^{\lambda}.

We have
\mathtt{p}_{3},\mathtt{p}_{13},\mathtt{p}_{15},\mathtt{p}_{17}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
\mathtt{p}_{5},\mathtt{p}_{9},\mathtt{p}_{11}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
, but
\mathtt{p}_{13},\mathtt{p}_{15}
and
\mathtt{p}_{17}
have shifted residues, thus these numbers appear as indices in the front
\psi
-chain.
We will use two different expressions of
v_{\mathtt{T}}
throughout our proofs: in one the
\Psi
terms are enclosed by two individual
\psi
-chains (
eq.

3.1
) and in the other one we push the
\psi
terms appearing on the left as much to the right as possible (
eq.

3.2
):

(3.1)

\displaystyle v_{\mathtt{T}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{11}\psi_{10}\psi_{9}\psi_{8}\psi_{7}\psi_{12}\psi_{13}\psi_{14}\psi_{15}\Psi_{5}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{7}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{11}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{9}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{13}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{16}\psi_{15}\dots\psi_{10}z^{\lambda}

(3.2)

\displaystyle=\psi_{11}\psi_{10}\psi_{9}\psi_{8}\psi_{7}\Psi_{5}\psi_{12}\psi_{13}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{7}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{11}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{14}\psi_{15}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{9}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{13}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{16}\psi_{15}\dots\psi_{10}z^{\lambda}.

In general, we can write
eq.

3.1
as:

(3.3)

v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi_{w}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\downarrow}}\dots\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{a(-1)}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{d}}}{\downarrow}}(\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{a})z^{\lambda},

for some minimal length
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, where the bracketed terms only appear if
a
is even.
We will be using this bracket notation throughout our proofs.
(We will introduce a similar expression for
eq.

3.2
before
3.17
.)
When acting on
v_{\mathtt{T}}
by a generator
\psi_{k}
, we will consider
v_{\mathtt{T}}
in normal form as in
eq.

3.1
.
It will be useful to have compact notation for the front terms of
v_{\mathtt{T}}
that do not play a role in our computations.

Let
\psi^{*}
denote the leading terms (in the normal form for
\psi_{\mathtt{T}}
) that commute with
\psi_{k}
(
\psi^{*}
may be empty), so that
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
for some
\mathtt{T}^{\prime}\geq\mathtt{T}
, and
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
.
For example:

\displaystyle\psi_{12}v_{\mathtt{T}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{12}\psi_{10}\psi_{9}\psi_{8}\psi_{7}\Psi_{5}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{7}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{11}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{14}\psi_{15}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{9}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{13}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{16}\psi_{15}\dots\psi_{10}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\psi_{10}\psi_{9}\psi_{8}\psi_{7}\Psi_{5}}_{=\psi^{*}}\psi_{12}\underbrace{\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{7}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{11}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{14}\psi_{15}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{9}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{13}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{16}\psi_{15}\dots\psi_{10}z^{\lambda}}_{=v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{12}\Psi_{11}\underbrace{\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{7}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{9}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{14}\psi_{15}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{9}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{13}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{16}\psi_{15}\dots\psi_{10}z^{\lambda}}_{=v_{\mathtt{S}}\text{ for some }\mathtt{S}>\mathtt{T}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{12}\Psi_{11}v_{\mathtt{S}}.

3.1.
Action of
\psi_{k}
with
k
even

The following statement describes the action of
y_{r}
for any
r
and
\psi_{k}
for
k
even (cf.
[
Spe14
, Proposition 5.4]
).

Proposition 3.11
.

Assume that
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
with corresponding
v_{\mathtt{T}}
.
Then

(1)

y_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=-y_{r-1}v_{\mathtt{T}}=v_{s_{r-1}\mathtt{T}}
if
r-1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T}),r\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
r
is odd;

(2)

y_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
otherwise;

(3)

\psi_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}
if
r+1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T}),r\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
r
is even;

(4)

\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
for all other even
k
, unless
\text{\boldmath$i$}_{\mathtt{p}_{k+1}}=(0,1)
,
\mathtt{p}_{k+1}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
.

Proof.

Cases
1
and
2
follow immediately from
3.4
.

For the third statement, if
r\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
and
r-1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
, then
\psi_{r-1}v_{\mathtt{T}}
is already in reduced form as
w^{\mathtt{T}}<w^{s_{r-1}\mathtt{T}}
.

For case
4
, if
k
is in
\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
and
k+1
is in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
, we have that
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}=\psi^{*}\psi_{k}^{2}v_{s_{k}\mathtt{T}^{\prime}}=0
as
i_{k}=i_{k+1}
in the tableau
s_{k}\mathtt{T}^{\prime}
.

Finally, assume that
\mathtt{p}_{k+1}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
or
\mathtt{p}_{k+1}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
, that is
k
and
k+1
are adjacent in
\mathtt{T}
.
To prove case 4
\psi_{k}
on
v_{\mathtt{T}}
, we have five cases to consider:

(A)

\psi_{k}
commutes with
\psi_{\mathtt{T}}
;

(B_{r})

\text{\boldmath$i$}_{\mathtt{p}_{k+1}}=(1,0)
,
\mathtt{p}_{k+1}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
(i.e. the first term that
\psi_{k}
does not commute with is
\Psi_{k-1}
and
\psi_{\mathtt{T}}
involves
r

\Psi
terms unless
r=0
);

(C_{r})

\text{\boldmath$i$}_{\mathtt{p}_{k+1}}=(1,0)
,
\mathtt{p}_{k+1}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
(i.e. the first term that
\psi_{k}
does not commute with is
\Psi_{k+1}
and
\psi_{\mathtt{T}}
involves
r

\Psi
terms unless
r=0
);

(D_{s})

\text{\boldmath$i$}_{\mathtt{p}_{k+1}}=(0,1)
,
\mathtt{p}_{k+1}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
(i.e. the first term that
\psi_{k}
does not commute with is
\psi_{k-1}
and
\ell(w^{\mathtt{T}^{\prime}})=s
);

(E)

\text{\boldmath$i$}_{\mathtt{p}_{k+1}}=(0,1)
,
\mathtt{p}_{k+1}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
(i.e. the first term that
\psi_{k}
does not commute with is
\psi_{k+1}
).

Note
: this is a special case which will be proved later in
3.13
.

Case
A

Follows immediately from
3.1
.

Cases
B
and
C

The proof is essentially the same as
[
Spe14
, Proposition 5.4]
.
We use induction on
r
, the number of
\Psi
terms appearing in
v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
.
Note that
B_{0}
can only occur if
\mathtt{T}=\mathtt{T}^{\lambda}
and
C_{0}
can only occur if
\mathtt{T}=\mathtt{T}^{\lambda}
where
a
is odd or
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}
where
a
is even and
a\leq\ell\leq n
(recall that
\ell=\mathtt{T}(1,a)
).
In the latter case if
k<\ell
we have that:

\displaystyle\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{k+2}(\psi_{k}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k})\psi_{k-1}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{k+2}(\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}\psi_{k+1})\psi_{k-1}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=0.

If
k>\ell
, then we are in case
A
.

For
B_{r}
we have that

\displaystyle\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}\psi^{*}\Psi_{k-1}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}(\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k})\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}(\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1})v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2}v_{\mathtt{S}}=0\text{ by $B_{r-1}$ or $A$}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}-\psi^{*}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}y_{k-2}v_{\mathtt{S}}\\
+2\psi^{*}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}y_{k-1}v_{\mathtt{S}}\\
-\psi^{*}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}y_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}\\
\end{cases}=0\text{ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{standard-y}}

\displaystyle=0.

Similarly, for
C_{r}
we have that

\displaystyle\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}\psi^{*}\Psi_{k+1}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}(\psi_{k}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k})\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+1}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}(\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+1})v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k+2}v_{\mathtt{S}}=0\text{ by $C_{r-1}$ or $A$}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}+\psi^{*}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}y_{k+1}v_{\mathtt{S}}\\
-2\psi^{*}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}y_{k+2}v_{\mathtt{S}}\\
+\psi^{*}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}y_{k+3}v_{\mathtt{S}}\end{cases}=0\text{ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{standard-y}}

\displaystyle=0.

Case
D

We prove the statement by induction on
s
, the length of
\psi_{\mathtt{T}^{\prime}}
.
Details can be included by switching the toggle in the
arXiv
version of this paper.
∎

The next lemma is very similar to
[
Spe14
, Lemma 5.5]
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Suppose
k
is odd,
v_{\mathtt{T}}
is as in
eq.

3.2
and let
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
be some permutation with corresponding reduced expression
\psi^{*}
with which
\psi_{k}
commutes.
(Note that in all cases below we have that
\mathtt{T}^{\prime}\ledom\mathtt{S}
and that
\ell(w^{\mathtt{T}})=\ell(w^{\mathtt{U}})+4
or
\ell(w^{\mathtt{T}})=\ell(w^{\mathtt{S}})+8
.)

(1)

If
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
where
v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}=\Psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{U}}
, then
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=-2\psi_{k}\psi^{*}v_{\mathtt{U}}
(compare with
3.4
);

(2)

If
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
where
v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}=\Psi_{k+2}\Psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}
, then
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi_{k}\psi^{*}v_{\mathtt{S}}
,

(3)

If
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
where
v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}=\Psi_{k-2}\Psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}
, then
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi_{k}\psi^{*}v_{\mathtt{S}}
,

Proof.

1)

If
v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
has
\Psi_{k}
on the left, we see that this means that
k-1,k\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T}^{\prime})
and
k+1,k+2\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T}^{\prime})
, so the result follows by
3.6
.

2)

If
v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
has
\Psi_{k+2}\Psi_{k}
on the left, we see that this means that
k-1,k,k+1,k+2\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T}^{\prime})
and
k+3,k+4\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T}^{\prime})
, so we have that:

\displaystyle\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}\psi^{*}\Psi_{k+2}\Psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+3}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+3}(\psi_{k}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k})\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+3}(\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}\psi_{k+1})\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle+\psi^{*}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+3}(y_{k})\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}-2\psi^{*}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+3}(y_{k+1})\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}\\
+\psi^{*}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+3}(y_{k+2})\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}\end{cases}=0\text{ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{standard-y}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+3}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}(\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+1})\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle+\psi^{*}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+3}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+3}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}(\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k+2})\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle+\psi^{*}(\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+3}\psi_{k+2})\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\Psi_{k+2}\Psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle+\psi^{*}(\psi_{k+3}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+3}+y_{k+2}-2y_{k+3}+y_{k+4})\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\Psi_{k+2}\Psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle+\psi^{*}\psi_{k+3}\psi_{k+2}\underbrace{\psi_{k+3}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}}_{=0\text{ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{even} case $4$}}

\displaystyle+\psi^{*}(y_{k+2})\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}-2\psi^{*}(y_{k+3})\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}\\
+\psi^{*}(y_{k+4})\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}\end{cases}=0\text{ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{standard-y}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}\psi^{*}v_{\mathtt{S}}+\psi^{*}\Psi_{k+2}\Psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}v_{\mathtt{S}}

3)

Similarly, if
v_{\mathtt{T}}
has
\Psi_{k-2}\Psi_{k}
on the left, we see that this means that
k-3,k-2\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T}^{\prime})
and
k-1,k,k+1,k+2\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T}^{\prime})
.
Then:

\displaystyle\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}\psi^{*}\Psi_{k-2}\Psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-3}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-3}(\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k})\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-3}(\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1})\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}-\psi^{*}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-3}(y_{k-1})\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}\\
+2\psi^{*}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-3}(y_{k})\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}\end{cases}=0\text{ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{standard-y}}

\displaystyle-\psi^{*}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-3}(y_{k+1})\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-3}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}(\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1})\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle+\psi^{*}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-3}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-3}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}(\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2})\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle+\psi^{*}(\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-3}\psi_{k-2})\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\Psi_{k-2}\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle+\psi^{*}(\psi_{k-3}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-3}-y_{k-3}+2y_{k-2}-y_{k-1})\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\Psi_{k-2}\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle+\psi^{*}\underbrace{(0-0+0-y_{k-1})}_{\text{by \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{even} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{standard-y}}}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\Psi_{k-2}\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}v_{\mathtt{S}}+\psi^{*}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

In order to prove the results in the second and third assertions, we will show that

\displaystyle\Psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}v_{\mathtt{S}}=0.

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt(B)

\displaystyle\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}v_{\mathtt{S}}=0,

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt(C)

where
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\Psi_{k+2}\Psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}
(case 2) and
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\Psi_{k-2}\Psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}
(case 3) for
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
.
We will do this by simultaneous induction on
r
, the number of
\Psi
terms appearing in
v_{\mathtt{S}}
.

Suppose we can write
v_{\mathtt{S}}
above as
v_{\mathtt{S}}=\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}\dots\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{a(-1)}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{d}}}{\downarrow}}(\psi_{\ell-1}\psi_{\ell-2}\dots\psi_{a+1}\psi_{a})z^{\lambda}
.
We also denote
v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(2)}}}=\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\downarrow}}\dots\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{a(-1)}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{d}}}{\downarrow}}(\psi_{\ell-1}\psi_{\ell-2}\dots\psi_{a+1}\psi_{a})z^{\lambda}
. (Recall the bracket notation from
eq.

3.3
.)

Let
B_{r}
and
C_{r}
denote that statements
B
and
C
hold if
v_{\mathtt{S}}
contains
r

\Psi
terms.

First, we prove that
(B_{r})
follows if both
(B_{s})
and
(C_{s})
hold for all
s<r
.
For
B_{0}
,

i)

if
a
is odd, then

\Psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}z^{\lambda}=\psi_{k}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k}z^{\lambda}=0

as at least one of
\psi_{k}
and
\psi_{k+2}
kills
z^{\lambda}
; and

ii)

if
a
is even, then

\displaystyle\Psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{k}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{k+4}(\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+3}\psi_{k+2})\psi_{k+1}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{k}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{k+4}(\psi_{k+3}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+3})\psi_{k+1}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle+\Psi_{k}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{k+4}y_{k+2}\psi_{k+1}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle-2\Psi_{k}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{k+4}y_{k+3}\psi_{k+1}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle+\Psi_{k}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{k+4}y_{k+4}\psi_{k+1}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=0.

Next assume
r>0
and that
B_{s}
holds for all
s<r
.

a)

Let
y_{1}\geq k+6
. As
\mathtt{T}
is standard, this is only possible if
\Psi_{k+2}\Psi_{k}
formed the chain on the right of
\Psi_{y_{1}}
, hence we must have that
m_{1}=k+2
, then

\Psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}v_{\mathtt{S}}=\Psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(2)}}}=\Psi_{y_{1}}\Psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}-2}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(2)}}}=0\text{ by $B_{r-1}$.}

b)

Let
y_{1}=k+4
.
Once again
m_{1}=k+2
, hence

\displaystyle\Psi_{k}(\psi_{k+2}\Psi_{k+4}\Psi_{k+2})\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+4}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(2)}}}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{k}(\psi_{k+2}+\Psi_{k+4}\Psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+4})v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(2)}}}\text{ by our previous result}

\displaystyle=0\text{ by $B_{r-2}$.}

c)

Finally, let
y_{1}=k-2
.
We immediately see that in this case
y_{2}\geq k+4
(as
v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}=\Psi_{k+2}\Psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}
, that is
\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+2}}{\downarrow}}
sat on top of the first
\Psi
chain).
Let
v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(3)}}}=\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{3}}}{\downarrow}}\dots\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{a(-1)}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{d}}}{\downarrow}}(\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{a})z^{\lambda}
.
Then

\displaystyle\Psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+6}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\downarrow}}\Psi_{k+4}\Psi_{k+2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(3)}}}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{k}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+6}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}(\psi_{k+2}\Psi_{k+4}\Psi_{k+2})\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(3)}}}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{k}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+6}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}(\psi_{k+2}+\Psi_{k+4}\underbrace{\Psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+4})\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(3)}}}}_{=0\text{ by $B_{s}$ for some $s<r$}}

\displaystyle=\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+6}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\downarrow}}\Psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(3)}}}

\displaystyle=\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+6}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k+2}(\psi_{k}\Psi_{k-2}\Psi_{k})\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(3)}}}

\displaystyle=\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+6}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k+2}\underbrace{(\psi_{k}+\Psi_{k-2}\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2})}_{\text{ by our previous result}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(3)}}}

\displaystyle=\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+6}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(3)}}}

\displaystyle+\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+6}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k+2}\Psi_{k-2}\underbrace{\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(3)}}}}_{=0\text{ by $C_{s}$ for some $s<r$}}

\displaystyle=\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+6}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(3)}}}

\displaystyle=0\text{ by $B_{s}$ for some $s<r$.}

Now we prove that
(C_{r})
holds if
(B_{s})
and
(C_{s})
hold for all
s<r
.
For
C_{0}
,

i)

if
a
is odd, then

\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}z^{\lambda}=\psi_{k}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k}z^{\lambda}=0

since at least one of
\psi_{k}
and
\psi_{k-2}
kills
z^{\lambda}
; and

ii)

if
a
is even, then

\displaystyle\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{k}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots(\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2})\psi_{k-3}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{k}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots(\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1})\psi_{k-3}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle-\Psi_{k}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots y_{k-2}\psi_{k-3}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle+2\Psi_{k}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots y_{k-1}\psi_{k-3}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle-\Psi_{k}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots y_{k}\psi_{k-3}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=0.

Next assume
r>0
and that
C_{s}
holds for all
s<r
.
Let
v_{\mathtt{S}},v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(2)}}}
,
v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(3)}}}
be as above and write
v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}=\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{4}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{4}}}{\downarrow}}\dots\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{a(-1)}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{d}}}{\downarrow}}(\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{a})z^{\lambda}
.
As
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
and
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\Psi_{k-2}\Psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}
, we immediately see that
v_{\mathtt{S}}
starts with
\Psi_{k-4}
,
\Psi_{k-2}
or some
\Psi_{y}
such that
y\geq k+2
.

a)

If
y_{1}\geq k+2
, then
m_{1}=k+2
by a similar reasoning as above, hence

\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}v_{\mathtt{S}}=0,

as in this case
\psi_{k-2}
commutes with everything to its left because the lowest indexed
\Psi
-term in
v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(2)}}}
is
\Psi_{k+4}
.

b)

Let
y_{1}=k-4
.
We immediately see that in this case
y_{2}=k-2
, as
v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}=\Psi_{k-2}\Psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}
.
Here we have two subcases to consider.

i)

y_{3}\geq k+2
:

\displaystyle\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}\Psi_{k-4}\Psi_{k-2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{3}}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{k}\underbrace{(\psi_{k-2}+\Psi_{k-4}\Psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-4})}_{\text{by our previous result}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{3}}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{3}}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}

\displaystyle+\Psi_{k}\Psi_{k-4}\Psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-4}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{3}}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}=0\text{ by $C_{r-2}$}

\displaystyle=\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+4}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{3}}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k-2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{k}\Psi_{k+2}\Psi_{k}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}

\displaystyle=\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+4}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{3}}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k-2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\underbrace{(\psi_{k}+\Psi_{k+2}\Psi_{k}\psi_{k+2})}_{\text{by our previous result}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}

\displaystyle=\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+4}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{3}}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k-2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{k}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}

\displaystyle+\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+4}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{3}}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k-2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi_{k+2}\underbrace{\Psi_{k}\psi_{k+2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}}_{=0\text{ $B_{s}$ for some $s<r$}}

\displaystyle=\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+4}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{3}}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}

\displaystyle=0\text{ by $C_{s}$ for some $s<r$.}

ii)

y_{3}=k
:

\displaystyle\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}\Psi_{k-4}\Psi_{k-2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{k}\underbrace{(\psi_{k-2}+\Psi_{k-4}\Psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-4})}_{\text{by our previous result}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}

\displaystyle+\Psi_{k}\Psi_{k-4}\Psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-4}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}=0\text{ by $C_{r-2}$}

\displaystyle=\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{k}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{k}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}

\displaystyle=-2\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{k}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{k}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}\text{ by case $A$}

\displaystyle=-2\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-6}}{\downarrow}}\Psi_{k}\psi_{k-2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-4}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}v_{{\mathtt{S}_{(4)}}}

\displaystyle=0\text{ by $C_{s}$ for some $s<r$.}\qed

Now we are ready to prove the remaining case from
3.11
:

Proposition 3.13
.

If
k
is even and
k
and
k+1
are adjacent in
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
, then
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
.

Proof.

By
3.11
, we only need to consider the case when
\mathtt{p}_{k+1}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
and
\text{\boldmath$i$}_{\mathtt{p}_{k+1}}=(0,1)
.

Let
m
be minimal such that
\mathtt{T}(1,m-1)\geq m+1
and
\mathtt{T}(1,m)\geq m+2
for
m
odd and let
E_{m}
denote that the statement holds if
\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{x}}{\downarrow}}
is the first
\Psi
chain that
\psi_{k}
does not commute with.
We will proceed by reverse induction on
m
.
Details can be included by switching the toggle in the
arXiv
version of this paper.
∎

3.2.
Three consecutive elements in
\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
or
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})

From now on, we will be working with
eq.

3.2
.
In order to fully describe the action of the
\psi
-generators on the basis elements
v_{\mathtt{T}}
, we still need to consider the cases when the first element in
v_{\mathtt{T}}
that
\psi_{k}
does not commute with is
\psi_{k-1}
or
\psi_{k+1}
.
We will do this in the next two statements where we also give examples of the types of tableaux corresponding to such expressions.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
k
be odd and
\mathtt{T},\mathtt{T}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
with corresponding
v_{\mathtt{T}},v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
.

•

If
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2}(\psi_{k-3}\dots\psi_{x+2})\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m-2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{x}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
where
x\leq k-4
and
y_{1}\geq k+2
, then
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m-2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{x}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
.

•

If
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k+2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m-2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
where
y_{1}\geq k+4
, then
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\psi_{k+2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m-2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
k
be odd
\mathtt{T},\mathtt{T}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
with corresponding
v_{\mathtt{T}},v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
.

•

If
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2}(\psi_{k-3}\dots\psi_{x+2})\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{x}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
where
x\leq k-4
and
y_{1}\geq k+2
, then
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\psi_{k-2}(\psi_{k-3}\dots\psi_{x+2})\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{x}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
.

•

If
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k+2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
where
y_{1}\geq k+4
, then
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\psi_{k+2}\psi_{k+1}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
.

Remarks
.

Observe that
3.14
can be visualised the following way: if
k-1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
,
k,k+1,k+2\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
i_{k-1}=i_{k}
, then
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=v_{\mathtt{S}}
where
\mathtt{S}
is the tableau with
k-1,k,k+1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{S}),k+2\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{S})
and outside of these entries it agrees with
\mathtt{T}
.

Similarly,
3.15
corresponds to the case when
k-1,k,k+1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T}),k+2\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
i_{k+1}=i_{k+2}
. Then
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=v_{\mathtt{S}}
where
\mathtt{S}
is the tableau with
k,k+1,k+2\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{S}),k-1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{S})
and outside of these entries it agrees with
\mathtt{T}
.

Example 3.16
.

Let
\lambda=(8,1^{6})
and take

\mathtt{T}=\begin{array}[]{l}\qquad\hbox to127.47pt{\vbox to112.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.32607pt\lower-92.87146pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}

Proof of
3.14
.

We have two options:
i_{k-1}=0=i_{k}
or
i_{k-1}=1=i_{k}
.
Fix some odd
m<a
.

Case
1
:
i_{k-1}=0=i_{k}

Assume that
\mathtt{T}(1,m)=k
. In this case
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2}\linebreak(\psi_{k-3}\dots\psi_{x+2})\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m-2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{x}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}\dots\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{a(-1)}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{d}}}{\downarrow}}(\psi_{\ell}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{a})z^{\lambda}
(here
x\leq k-4
and
y_{1}\geq k+2
).

\displaystyle\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}\psi^{*}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2}(\psi_{k-3}\dots\psi_{x+2})\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m-2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{x}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}\dots\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{a(-1)}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{d}}}{\downarrow}}(\psi_{\ell}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{a})z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2}\Psi_{k}\underbrace{(\psi_{k-3}\dots\psi_{x+2})\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m-2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{x}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}\dots\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{a(-1)}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{d}}}{\downarrow}}(\psi_{\ell}\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{a})z^{\lambda}}_{\scriptsize v_{\mathtt{S}}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}(\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k})\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}(\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}\underbrace{\psi_{k-1})\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}}_{=0\text{ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{even}}}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}-\psi^{*}(y_{k-1})\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}\\
+2\psi^{*}(y_{k})\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}\\
\end{cases}=0\text{ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{standard-y}}

\displaystyle-\psi^{*}(y_{k+1})\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\psi_{k-2}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}

Case
2
:
i_{k-1}=1=i_{k}

Assume that
\mathtt{T}(1,m-3)=k
, then
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}\psi_{k+1}\linebreak\psi_{k+2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m-2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}\dots\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{a(-1)}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{d}}}{\downarrow}}(\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{a})z^{\lambda}
(here
y_{1}\geq k+4
and
x<y_{1}
).

\displaystyle\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}\psi^{*}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}\psi_{k+1}(\psi_{x}\dots\psi_{k+3})\psi_{k+2}\underbrace{\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m-2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}\dots\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{a(-1)}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{d}}}{\downarrow}}(\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{a})z^{\lambda}}_{v_{\mathtt{S}}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}(\psi_{k}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k})\psi_{k+1}(\psi_{x}\dots\psi_{k+3})\psi_{k+2}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}(\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}\underbrace{\psi_{k-1})\psi_{k+1}(\psi_{x}\dots\psi_{k+3})\psi_{k+2}v_{\mathtt{S}}}_{=0\text{ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{even}}}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}-\psi^{*}(y_{k-1})\psi_{k+1}(\psi_{x}\dots\psi_{k+3})\psi_{k+2}v_{\mathtt{S}}\\
+2\psi^{*}(y_{k})\psi_{k+1}(\psi_{x}\dots\psi_{k+3})\psi_{k+2}v_{\mathtt{S}}\end{cases}=0\text{ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{standard-y}}

\displaystyle-\psi^{*}(y_{k+1})\psi_{k+1}(\psi_{x}\dots\psi_{k+3})\psi_{k+2}v_{\mathtt{S}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}(\psi_{x}\dots\psi_{k+3})\psi_{k+2}v_{\mathtt{S}}

Note that if
y_{1}=k+2
, we have that
v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\psi_{k-1}\psi_{k}\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k+2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m-2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k-2}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{k+3}\psi_{k+4}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m+2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{k+2}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m+4}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\downarrow}}\dots\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{a(-1)}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{d}}}{\downarrow}}(\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{a})z^{\lambda}
, but the proof is identical.
∎

Proof of
3.15
.

Similar to the proof of
3.14
.
Details can be included by switching the toggle in the
arXiv
version of this paper.
∎

3.3.
Two consecutive pairs in
\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
or
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})

Now, we have all the tools necessary to prove the analogous statements of Corollaries
3.21
and
3.25
in
[
Spe15
]
for all
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
.

For the rest of proofs in this section, we will be working with
eq.

3.2
.
To further simplify notation, we let
\chi_{i}
denote the terms sandwiched between the
\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{i}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{i}}}{\downarrow}}
-terms, that is
\chi_{i}
corresponds to the individual
\psi
terms between
\psi_{m_{i}}
and
\psi_{y_{i}}
.
If
i=1
,
\chi_{1}
denotes the first
\psi
-chain in
v_{\mathtt{T}}
on the left.
For example (compare with
eq.

3.2
):

\displaystyle v_{\mathtt{T}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{y_{1}+1}\psi_{y_{1}+3}\psi_{y_{1}+2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{y_{2}+2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\downarrow}}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{3}}}{\downarrow}}\psi_{y_{4}+1}\psi_{y_{4}+2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{4}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{4}}}{\downarrow}}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}

(3.4)

\displaystyle=\chi_{1}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\downarrow}}\chi_{2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\downarrow}}\chi_{3}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{3}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{3}}}{\downarrow}}\chi_{4}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{4}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{4}}}{\downarrow}}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}},

where
v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}
corresponds to the rest of
v_{\mathtt{T}}
on the right (it is in reduced form and shorter than
v_{\mathtt{T}}
).
Here we have

•

\chi_{1}=\psi_{y_{1}+1}\psi_{y_{1}+3}\psi_{y_{1}+2}
;

•

\chi_{2}=\psi_{y_{2}+2}
;

•

\chi_{3}=\operatorname{id}
; and

•

\chi_{4}=\psi_{y_{4}+1}\psi_{y_{4}+2}
.

Unless
\chi_{i}=\operatorname{id}
, we define
\chi_{i}(\mathfrak{l})
to be the leftmost
\psi
-term in
\chi_{i}
.
Note that
\chi_{i}(\mathfrak{l})=\psi_{y_{i}+2}
.
We also set
\chi_{i}(\mathfrak{f})=\psi_{y_{i}+1}
(
\chi_{i}(\mathfrak{f})
might not appear).
Also, consistently with this new notation, if
a
is even, we write
\chi_{\ell}=\psi_{\ell-1}\dots\psi_{a}
.

The next proposition is the analogous version of
[
Spe15
, Corollary 3.17]
.

Proposition 3.17
.

Suppose
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
and
1\leq k\leq n-1
is odd.

(1)

If
\mathtt{p}_{k},\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
and
\mathtt{p}_{c}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
is minimal such that
c>k+2
and the entries
k+3,\dots,c-1
are all unpaired, then
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}
where
\mathtt{S}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
agrees with
\mathtt{T}
outside of these three pairs, but has
\mathtt{p}_{k}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{S})
and
\mathtt{p}_{k+2},\mathtt{p}_{c}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{S})
.

(2)

If
\mathtt{p}_{k},\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
\mathtt{p}_{c}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
is maximal such that
c<k
and the entries
c+1,\dots,k-2
are all unpaired, then
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{S}}
where
\mathtt{S}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
agrees with
\mathtt{T}
outside of these three pairs, but has
\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{S})
and
