Corollary 3.28
.

Assume
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
with
\mathtt{p}_{k},\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
.

a)

If
\text{\boldmath$i$}_{\mathtt{p}_{k}}=(1,0)
, let
v_{\mathtt{T}}
have reverse normal form

v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\rho_{1}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\uparrow}}\rho_{2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\uparrow}}\dots\rho{h}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{h}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{h}}}{\uparrow}}(\rho_{d})z^{\lambda}.

Suppose that
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
and that
t
is minimal such that
y_{t}>k-4t
or
y_{t}=k-4t+2
and
\rho_{t}=\operatorname{id}
.

b)

If
\text{\boldmath$i$}_{\mathtt{p}_{k}}=(0,1)
, let
v_{\mathtt{T}}
have reverse normal form

v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi^{*}\rho_{0}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{0}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{0}}}{\uparrow}}\rho_{1}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{1}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{1}}}{\uparrow}}\rho_{2}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{2}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{2}}}{\uparrow}}\dots\rho_{h}\Psi\hskip-4.0pt\underset{\scriptscriptstyle{y_{h}}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{m_{h}}}{\uparrow}}(\rho_{d})z^{\lambda}.

Suppose that
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
and that
t
is minimal such that
y_{t}>k-4t-2
or
y_{t}=k-4t
and
\rho_{t}=\operatorname{id}
.

Then there exists some minimal
r
such that
\mathtt{T}
is as in
3.26
and
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi_{k}v_{(\mathtt{T})_{k+2-2r,k+2}}
which is in reduced form.

Proof.

Just as
3.21
, the result follows immediately from
3.25
.
∎

If we have a tableau
\mathtt{T}
with
\mathtt{p}_{k},\mathtt{p}_{k+2}
in the arm or in the leg, we can easily calculate whether
\psi_{k}
rotates the dominoes as given in
3.26
and
3.19
or if
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
.
Assume that
\mathtt{p}_{k},\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
and that we wish to determine the action of
\psi_{k}
on
v_{\mathtt{T}}
.
We start counting the pairs in the natural order (starting with
\mathtt{p}_{k}
).
If we have counted
m+1
pairs in the leg and
m
in the arm, we stop and perform the rotation as given in
3.21
.
If we reach the highest indexed pair without ever satisfying this condition,
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
.

Similarly, assume that
\mathtt{p}_{k},\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and consider the expression
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}
.
We start counting the pairs backwards from
\mathtt{p}_{k+2}
.
If at any point we have counted
m+1
pairs in the arm and
m
in the leg, we stop and perform the rotation as given in
3.28
.
If we arrive at the lowest indexed pair without ever satisfying this condition, once again we see that
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
.
Note that the highest (resp. lowest) indexed pair is not necessarily
\mathtt{p}_{n}
(resp.
\mathtt{p}_{3}
).

Proposition 3.29
.

The algorithms above give the correct reduced form for
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}
.

Proof.

We start with the first algorithm.
The proof itself is just a translation between the algorithm and
3.21
.
Assume that
y_{1}
is minimal such that
m_{1}\leq k\leq y_{1}
, that is the first pair the algorithm counts in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
is
\mathtt{p}_{y_{1}}
.

When we get to the
r
th pair in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
, we must have counted at least
r+2
in
\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
, otherwise the algorithm would terminate.
Thus we see that we counted at least
2r+2
pairs in total, starting with
\mathtt{p}_{k}
and so
y_{r}\geq 4r+k
or
y_{r}=4r+k-2
and
\chi_{r}\neq\operatorname{id}
.

If the algorithm gets to the final pair without ever satisfying the condition that we have counted one more pair in
\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
than in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
, it is easy to see that the above inequality (resp. equality) holds for all
r
, so we know by
3.18
that
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
.
Otherwise, if we counted
f
many pairs in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
f+1
in
\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
, then by
3.21
, we have the desired result.

The case when
y_{1}
is minimal such that
m_{1}\leq k+2\leq y_{1}
and the second algorithm can be proved analogously.
∎

At last, we have completely determined the action of the different generators on the basis elements
v_{\mathtt{T}}
of
S^{\lambda}
.
We summarise our results in the next statement.

Proposition 3.30
.

Let
\lambda=(a,1^{b})
,
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
with corresponding
v_{\mathtt{T}}
.

(1)

e(\text{\boldmath$j$})v_{\mathtt{T}}=\delta_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}},\text{\boldmath$j$}}v_{\mathtt{T}}

(2)

y_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\begin{cases}-v_{s_{k}\mathtt{T}}&\text{ if $k$ is odd, $i_{k}=i_{k+1}$, $k\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})$, and $k+1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})$,}\\[5.0pt]
v_{s_{k-1}\mathtt{T}}&\text{ if $k$ is odd, $i_{k-1}=i_{k}$, $k-1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})$, and $k\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})$,}\\[5.0pt]
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(3)

For
k
even:

\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\begin{cases}v_{s_{k}\mathtt{T}}&\text{ if $k\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})$, and $k+1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})$},\\[5.0pt]
0&\text{ otherwise.}\\
\end{cases}

(4)

For
k
odd:

\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\begin{cases}v_{s_{k}\mathtt{T}}&\text{ if $1<k\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})$, and $k+1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})$},\\[5.0pt]
-2v_{s_{k-1}s_{k+1}s_{k}\mathtt{T}}&\text{ if $\mathtt{p}_{k}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T}),\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})$ and $\text{\boldmath$i$}_{\mathtt{p}_{k}}=\text{\boldmath$i$}_{\mathtt{p}_{k+2}}$},\\[5.0pt]
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=v_{\mathtt{S}}&\text{ if $k-1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})$, $k,k+1,k+2\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})$,}\\
&\text{ $i_{k-1}=i_{k}$ and $\mathtt{S}$ is as in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{halfdom-arm}},\\[5.0pt]
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=v_{\mathtt{P}}&\text{ if $k-1,k,k+1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T}),k+2\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})$,}\\
&\text{ $i_{k+1}=i_{k+2}$ and $\mathtt{P}$ is as in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{halfdom-leg}},\\[5.0pt]
\psi_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi_{r}v_{(\mathtt{T})^{2r,k}}&\text{ if $\mathtt{p}_{k},\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})$ and $\mathtt{T}$ satisfies}\\
&\text{ the conditions of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{pair-leg},}\\[5.0pt]
\psi_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=\psi_{r}v_{(\mathtt{T})_{2r,k}}&\text{ if $\mathtt{p}_{k},\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})$ and $\mathtt{T}$ satisfies}\\
&\text{ the conditions of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{pair-arm},}\\[5.0pt]
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

4.
Homomorphisms into
S^{\lambda}

As before, let
\lambda=(a,1^{b})
and fix
\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{Z})
a partition of
n
.
If there exists a nonzero homomorphism
\varphi:S^{\mu}\rightarrow S^{\lambda}
and
\varphi(z^{\mu})=v=\sum c_{\mathtt{T}}v_{\mathtt{T}}
, then
c_{\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
if and only if
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}
.
We call a node
(a,b)\in\mu
an
arm node
(resp.
leg node
) with respect to
\mathtt{T}
if
c_{\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
and
\mathtt{T}^{\mu}(i,j)
is in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
(resp. in
\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
).

4.1.
A novel way to label hook partitions

In this section, we prove some results that will be used in the proof of our main theorem.
In particular, we consider the action of the
y
and
\psi
generators on the basis elements
v_{\mathtt{T}}\in S^{\lambda}
.
In order to do this, we introduce an usual, albeit extremely important set of partitions and a rule to assign a coefficient to each.
Then, we show that the sum of the coefficients of the partitions in this set always equals to a certain constant (
4.11
).
Next, we introduce a convenient way to label tableaux of hook shapes whose arms contain only pairs and unpaired even numbers.
The rest of the section is dedicated to showing that if we label tableaux in this way, then the resulting labels and the set of partitions from earlier coincide and all
v_{\mathtt{T}}
involved in
v
is labelled by a tableaux of this form.

Lemma 4.1
.

If
v=\sum c_{\mathtt{T}}v_{\mathtt{T}}
, then
(J_{1}^{\mu}+J_{2}^{\mu})v=0
if and only if for any
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
with
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}
,
c_{\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
and any
1\leq a<k
for which
\mu_{a}
is even,
(a,\mu_{a})
is an arm node and
(a+1,1)
is a leg node.

Proof.

Let
v=\sum c_{\mathtt{T}}v_{\mathtt{T}}
and assume that
(J_{1}^{\mu}+J_{2}^{\mu})v=0
, then we have
y_{r}v=0
for all
1\leq r\leq n
.
By
3.4
,
y_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
unless
r
and
r+1
or
r
and
r-1
have the same residue.
We only show the case where
i_{r}=i_{r+1}
, the other has a very similar proof.
We can only have
i_{r}=i_{r+1}
if
r
and
r+1
are in different rows in
\mathtt{T}^{\mu}
, with
r
at the end of a row and
r+1
in the first node of the next row.
Now, this is only possible if the row containing
r
has even length, otherwise
r
and
r+1
have different residues and
y_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
for all such
r
.
If
i_{r}=i_{r+1}
with
r\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
and
r+1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
for some
\mathtt{T}
, then
y_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=-v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}
.
Then
y_{r}v\neq 0
, unless there is some
\mathtt{T}^{\prime}
involved in
v
that gives
y_{r}v_{\mathtt{T^{\prime}}}=v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}}
with
i_{r}=i_{r+1}
.
But this is impossible, hence if
J_{2}^{\mu}v=0
, we must have that whenever
i_{r}=i_{r+1}
then
r
is in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
or
r+1
is in
\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
. Furthermore, as
i_{r}=i_{r+1}
whenever
r
is in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
, we must have that
r+1
is in
\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
, because they cannot be next to each other with the same residue.

On the other hand, suppose that
r=\mathtt{T}^{\mu}(a,\mu_{a})
is an arm node and
r+1=\mathtt{T}^{\mu}(a+1,1)
is a leg node for every even length row
\mu_{a}
in
\mathtt{T}^{\mu}
with
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})
for all
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
with
c_{\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
.
Fix
\mathtt{T}
such that
c_{\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
for
1\leq r\leq n-1
.
If
r-1,r,r+1
are in different rows in
\mathtt{T}^{\mu}
, then
i_{r}\neq i_{r-1},i_{r+1}
, so by
3.4
,
y_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0

We immediately see that
y_{1}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
as
i_{1}\neq i_{2}
.
If
r
is at the end of an even length row in
\mathtt{T}^{\mu}
, then by our assumption
r
is in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
, hence
y_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0=y_{r+1}v_{\mathtt{T}}
.
Finally, if
n-1
and
n
are in the same row in
\mathtt{T}^{\mu}
, then
i_{n-1}\neq i_{n}
, so
y_{n}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
. If
n-1=\mathtt{T}^{\mu}(a-1,\mu_{a-1})
and
\mu_{a-1}
is odd, then
i_{n-1}\neq i_{n}
and if
\mu_{a-1}
, by our assumption
n-1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
n\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
.
Hence
y_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0=y_{r+1}v_{\mathtt{T}}
for all
\mathtt{T}
, thus it follows that
y_{r}v=0
for all
1\leq r\leq n
.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

If
v=\sum c_{\mathtt{T}}v_{\mathtt{T}}
such that
(J_{1}^{\mu}+J_{2}^{\mu})v=0
,
c_{\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
and
r,r+1,r+2\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
for
r
odd, then we must also have that
r-1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
.

Proof.

Assume otherwise, i.e.
r-1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
and
r,r+1,r+2\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
. Due to the parity conditions, we must have that
i_{r-1}=i_{r}
. Then by the previous lemma, we see that in this case we must have
r-1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
r\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
, a contradiction. Hence, if
c_{\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
and
r,r+1,r+2\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
, we have that
r-1
is also in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

If
v=\sum c_{\mathtt{T}}v_{\mathtt{T}}
such that
(J_{1}^{\mu}+J_{2}^{\mu})v=0
,
c_{\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
and
r-1,r,r+1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
for
r
odd, then
r+2\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
.

Proof.

Analogous to the proof of
4.2
.
∎

Recall the notion of a pair from
3.2
.

Definition 4.4
.

For
\lambda=(a,1^{b})
, we define the set of
pair-tableaux
of shape
\lambda
, denoted by
\mathsf{P}(\lambda)
, to be the set of tableaux in which if
k\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
for some even
2\leq k\leq n-1
, then we must have that
k+1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
and if
k\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
for some odd
3\leq k\leq n
, then we must have that
k-1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
.

The following is an immediate consequence of the previous three lemmas.

Corollary 4.5
.

Suppose
\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
and
\varphi(z^{\mu})=\sum c_{\mathtt{T}}v_{\mathtt{T}}
.
If
c_{\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
, then
\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{P}(\lambda)
.

Lemma 4.6
.

If
\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{P}(\lambda)
, then

i)

y_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
for all
1\leq k\leq n
,

ii)

\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
for all even
2\leq k\leq n-1
such that
k
and
k+1
are adjacent in
\mathtt{T}
, and

iii)

\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=v_{s_{k}\mathtt{T}}
for all even
2\leq k\leq n-1
such that
k\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
k+1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
.

Proof.

Follows immediately from the definition of
\mathsf{P}(\mathtt{T})
and
3.30
.
∎

Definition 4.7
.

Let
(\delta_{1}^{h_{1}},\delta_{2}^{h_{2}},\dots,\delta_{d}^{h_{d}})
be a weakly decreasing sequence of non-negative integers such that each
\delta_{i}
appears with multiplicity
h_{i}
.
We define the
delta (coefficient) function

\Delta(\delta_{1}^{h_{1}},\dots,\delta_{d}^{h_{d}}):=\prod_{1}^{d}\mathfrak{d}_{x}(\delta_{1}^{h_{1}},\dots,\delta_{d}^{h_{d}}).

where

\mathfrak{d}_{x}(\delta_{1}^{h_{1}},\dots,\delta_{d}^{h_{d}})=\binom{\delta_{x}-\mathcal{Y}+\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{X}},

and
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{X}
are determined by the following two rules:

(1)

If
\delta_{x-1}-\delta_{x}\geq h_{x}
, we set
\mathcal{X}:=h_{x}
(if
x=1
, we define
\mathcal{X}:=h_{1}
).
Otherwise, we find the maximal
1\leq y<x-1
such that
\delta_{y}-\delta_{x}\geq\sum_{y+1}^{x}h_{\rho}
and set
\mathcal{X}:=\sum_{y+1}^{x}h_{\rho}
.
If no such
y
exists, we set
\mathcal{X}:=\sum_{1}^{x}h_{\rho}
.

(2)

If
\delta_{y}-\delta_{z}\geq\sum_{y+1}^{z}h_{\rho}
for all
1\leq y\leq x
and
x+1\leq z\leq d
or
x=d
, we set
\mathcal{Y}:=0
.
Otherwise, we have to two cases to consider.

a)

If there exists some minimal
x+1\leq z\leq d
such that
\delta_{x}-\delta_{z}<\sum_{x+1}^{z}h_{\rho}
, we set
\mathcal{Y}:=\delta_{z}
.

b)

Otherwise, we find the maximal
1\leq y\leq x-1
and the minimal
x+1\leq z\leq d
such that
\delta_{y}-\delta_{z}<\sum_{y+1}^{z}h_{\rho}
and set
\mathcal{Y}:=\delta_{z}
.

Remark
.

It is easy to show that if 2a) fails, then the minimal
z
and maximal
y
satisfying 2b) are unique.

Example 4.8
.

For example, if we have the sequence
(13,10^{3},8^{2},6^{3},3^{2},2)
, then

\displaystyle\Delta(13,10^{3},8^{2},6^{3},3^{2},2)

\displaystyle=\prod_{x=1}^{6}\mathfrak{d}_{x}(13,10^{3},8^{2},6^{3},3^{2},2)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\binom{13-6+1}{1}}_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}}\underbrace{\binom{10-6+3}{3}}_{\mathfrak{d}_{2}}\underbrace{\binom{8-6+2}{2}}_{\mathfrak{d}_{3}}

\displaystyle\cdot\underbrace{\binom{6+9}{9}}_{\mathfrak{d}_{4}}\underbrace{\binom{3+2}{2}}_{\mathfrak{d}_{6}}\underbrace{\binom{2+1}{1}}_{\mathfrak{d}_{6}}.

In order to state the next definition, we need some additional notation.
For some
i\geq 0
and
v,s\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
g_{v},G_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},h_{v},H_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we set

\displaystyle\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{s},H_{s}}\quad}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{(i+G_{s},i+G_{s},\dots,i+G_{s}}_{H_{s}}),

\displaystyle\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{v},h_{v}}\quad}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{(i-g_{v},i-g_{v},\dots,i-g_{v}}_{h_{v}}).

As mentioned at the beginning of the section, we now introduce a set of partitions which will play a crucial rule when giving an explicit description of
\Hom(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
.

Definition 4.9
.

Fix
g_{v},G_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}{\geq 0}
such that
g_{v}<g_{v+1}
and
G_{s}<G_{s+1}
for all
1\leq v\leq\omega
and
1\leq s\leq\alpha
.
Let

\displaystyle\tau_{1}

\displaystyle=\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{2},H_{2}}\quad},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},\underbrace{i}_{j\text{th position}},

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1},h_{1}}\quad},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{2},h_{2}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big)

or

\displaystyle\tau_{2}

\displaystyle=\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{2},H_{2}}\quad},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},i,\underbrace{i}_{j\text{th position}},

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1},h_{1}}\quad},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{2},h_{2}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big)

be a partition with
R=1+\sum_{\rho=1}^{\alpha}H_{\rho}+\sum_{\rho=1}^{\omega}h_{\rho}
or
R=2+\sum_{\rho=1}^{\alpha}H_{\rho}+\sum_{\rho=1}^{\omega}h_{\rho}
parts and fix an integer
1\leq j\leq R
.
For
\tau_{(j,0)}:=\tau_{1}
or
\tau_{2}
, we define the set of partitions
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
as follows.
If
\tau_{j}=\tau_{j+1}
, we set
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}:=\varnothing
.
Otherwise, if
\tau_{(j,0)}=\tau_{1}
, define
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{\prime}:=\{\tau_{(j,0)},\tau_{(j,-1)},\tau_{(j,1)},\tau_{(j,2)},\dots,\tau_{(j,\omega+1)}\}
, where

\displaystyle\tau_{(j,-1)}

\displaystyle=\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},\underbrace{i-1}_{j\text{th position}},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1},h_{1}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big),

\displaystyle\tau_{(j,1)}

\displaystyle=\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},\underbrace{i-2}_{j\text{th position}},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1},h_{1}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big),

\displaystyle\tau_{(j,v+1)}

\displaystyle=\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1}+1,h_{1}}\quad},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{2}+1,h_{2}}\quad},\dots,

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{v-1}+1,h_{v-1}}\quad},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{v}+1,h_{v}+1}\quad},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{v+1},h_{v+1}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big).

(in
\tau_{(j,v+1)}
the leftmost
i-(g_{1}+1)
is in position
j
) and if
\tau_{(j,0)}=\tau_{1}
, define
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{\prime}:=\{\tau_{(j,0)},\newline
\tau_{(j,-1)},\tau_{(j,1)},\tau_{(j,2)},\dots,\tau_{(j,\omega+1)},\tau^{(j,1)},\tau^{(j,2)},\dots,\tau^{(j,\alpha+1)}\}
, where

\displaystyle\tau_{(j,-1)}

\displaystyle=\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},\underbrace{i}_{j-1\text{th position}},i-1,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1},h_{1}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big),

\displaystyle\tau_{(j,1)}

\displaystyle=\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},\underbrace{i}_{j-1\text{th position}},i-2,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1},h_{1}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big),

\displaystyle\tau_{(j,v+1)}

\displaystyle=\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},\underbrace{i}_{j-1\text{th position}},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1}+1,h_{1}}\quad},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{2}+1,h_{2}}\quad},\dots,

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{v-1}+1,h_{v-1}}\quad},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{v}+1,h_{v}+1}\quad},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{v+1},h_{v+1}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big),

\displaystyle\tau^{(j,1)}

\displaystyle=\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},\underbrace{i-1}_{j-1\text{th position}},i-1,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1},h_{1}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big),

\displaystyle\tau^{(j,s+1)}

\displaystyle=\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{s},H_{s}}\quad},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{s-1},H_{s-1}-1}\quad},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{s-2}-1,H_{s-2}}\quad},

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1}-1,H_{1}}\quad},i-1,\underbrace{i-1}_{j-1\text{th position}},i-1,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1},h_{1}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big).

Then we define
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
to be the set of all
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}^{\prime}
satisfying one of the following:

•

if
\tau=\tau_{(j,v+1)}
, then
g_{v}=\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}+1
and for each
1\leq r\leq\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}
, we have that
\tau_{j+r}\leq\tau_{j}-1-r
, or

•

if
\tau=\tau^{(j,s+1)}
then
G_{s+1}=\sum_{\rho=1}^{s}H_{\rho}+1
and for each
1\leq p\leq\sum_{\rho=1}^{s}H_{\rho}
, we have that
\tau_{j-1-p}\leq\tau_{j-1}+p
.

Remarks
.

We can visualise the set
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
as follows.
The Young diagrams of
\tau_{(j,1)}
and
\tau^{(j,1)}
are obtained from
\tau_{(j,0)}
by removing a horizontal (resp. vertical)
2
-strip, starting in the last node of row
\tau_{j}
.
(This implies that for
1\leq k
,
\tau_{(j,k)}\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
, i.e.
\tau_{(j,k)}
is a valid partition, if and only if
\tau_{j}\geq\tau_{j+1}+2
.
Similarly, for
1\leq s
,
\tau^{(j,s)}\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
if and only if
\tau_{j-1}=\tau_{j}
.)

The Young diagram of each
\tau_{(j,v+1)}
is obtained from
\tau_{(j,1)}
by removing a rim consisting of
2\cdot\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}
nodes, starting in row
j
and ending in the last node of row
j+\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}
.
Finally, the Young diagram of each
\tau^{(j,s+1)}
is obtained from
\tau_{(j,1)}
by removing a rim consisting of
2\cdot\sum_{\rho=1}^{s}h_{\rho}
nodes, starting at the end of row
j-2
and ending in the last node of row
j-1-\sum_{\rho=1}^{s-1}+1
).

In particular, every
\tau_{(j,v)}
and
\tau^{(j,s)}
can be obtained from
\tau_{(j,0)}
by removing a rim starting in the node
(j,\tau_{j})
and ending in a node which is in the set
\mathcal{R}:=\mathcal{R}_{1}\cup\mathcal{R}_{2}
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}:=

\displaystyle\{(j-m-1,\tau_{j}+m)\mid 0\leq m<m_{1}\leq j-2,(j-m_{1}-1,\tau_{j}+m_{1}+1)\not\in\tau_{(j,-1)}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2}:=

\displaystyle\{(j+m,\tau_{j}-m-1)\mid 0\leq m<m_{2}\leq R-j,(j+m_{2},\tau_{j}-m_{2})\not\in\tau_{(j,-1)}\}.

Example 4.10
.

Let
\tau_{(4,0)}=(10,8,7^{2},5,3^{2},2^{3})
, so we have that
[\tau_{(4,0)}]=

.

Next,
\tau_{(4,-1)}=(10,8,7,6,5,3^{2},2^{3})
, hence
[\tau_{(4,-1)}]=

where the node highlighted in red is node removed from
[\tau_{(j,0)}]
.

We can visualise the set
\mathcal{R}
as below.
In fact, we see that every blue node sits at the bottom of its column in
[\tau_{(j,-1)}]
and the set
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
contains seven partitions in total.
(Observe that if
\tau_{(j,0)}=\tau_{1}
, then row
j-1
already crosses the blue line, which is why we did not define any
\tau^{(j,s)}
in this case.)

In the following, we highlight removed nodes in blue.
First, we have
\tau_{(4,1)}=(10,8,7,5,5,3^{2},2^{3})
and
\tau^{(4,1)}=(10,8,6,6,5,3^{2},2^{3})
.
[\tau_{(4,1)}]=
[\tau^{(4,1)}]=

.

Then we have
\tau_{(4,2)}=(10,8,7,4^{2},3^{2},2^{3})
and
\tau_{(4,2)}=(10,8,7,4,2^{6})
.
[\tau_{(4,2)}]=
[\tau_{(4,3)}]=

.

Finally,
\tau^{(4,2)}=(10,6^{3},5,3^{2},2^{3})
.
[\tau^{(4,2)}]=

.

Remark
.

If
g_{v}=g_{v+1}-h_{v+1}
for all
1\leq v\leq z
and
G_{s+1}=G_{s}+H_{s}
for all
1\leq s\leq x
, then
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}=\{\tau_{(j,-1)},\tau_{(j,0)},\tau_{(j,1)},\tau_{(j,2)},\dots,\tau_{(j,z+1)},\tau^{(j,1)},\tau^{(j,2)},\dots,\tau^{(j,x+1)}\}
and

|\mathcal{T}_{i,j}|=2+z+1+x+1=x+z+4.

In particular, if
x=\alpha
and
z=\omega
and
H_{s}=1=h_{v}
for all
1\leq v\leq z
and
1\leq s\leq x
, then
x+z+4
is maximal.

The following statement shows that there is a special relation between the
\Delta
-coefficients of the partitions appearing in
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
where
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
is as in
4.9
.

Proposition 4.11
.

For any
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
with
1\leq j\leq R
and
i>1
, we have that

\sum_{v=0}^{z+1}\Delta(\tau_{(j,v)})+\sum_{s=1}^{x+1}\Delta(\tau^{(j,s)})=2\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)})

where
\tau_{(j,v)}
and
\tau^{(j,s)}
(
-1\leq v\leq z+1,1\leq s\leq x+1
) are as in
4.9
and
\Delta(\tau)
is computed according to
4.7
.

Proof.

We will prove the statement by considering the different sizes of
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
.
Abusing notation, if
\tau
is a partition of the form

\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},\underbrace{i}_{j\text{th position}},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1},h_{1}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big)

or

\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},i,\underbrace{i}_{j\text{th position}},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1},h_{1}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big)

we write
\Delta(\tau)=\mathfrak{d}_{G_{\alpha}}\dots\mathfrak{d}_{G_{1}}\mathfrak{d}(i)\mathfrak{d}_{g_{1}}\dots\mathfrak{d}_{g_{\omega}}
.
We also set

\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha}):=\mathfrak{d}_{G_{\alpha}}\dots\mathfrak{d}_{G_{1}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{d}(g_{1}^{\omega}):=\mathfrak{d}_{g_{1}}\dots\mathfrak{d}_{g_{\omega}}.

To simplify notation, we also write
i^{\prime}=i-\mathcal{Y}
.

The case when
|\mathcal{T}_{i,j}|=2

In this case
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}=\{\tau_{(j,0)},\tau_{(j,-1)}\}
.
Recall that if
\tau_{(j,0)}=\tau_{2}
, then
\tau^{(j,1)}\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
, so here we must have
\tau_{(j,0)}=\tau_{1}
.
Then

\displaystyle\tau_{(j,0)}

\displaystyle=\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},\underbrace{i}_{j\text{th position}},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1},h_{1}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big)

\displaystyle\tau_{(j,-1)}

\displaystyle=\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},\underbrace{i-1}_{j\text{th position}},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1},h_{1}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big)

where
G_{1}\geq 1
and
g_{1}=1
(since if
g_{1}\geq 2
, then
\tau_{(j,1)}\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
).
If
g_{1}=1=h_{1}
we have

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(j,0)})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})(i^{\prime}+1)((i-1)+1-\mathcal{Y})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=(i^{\prime}+1)\cdot i^{\prime}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

and

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\binom{(i-1)-\mathcal{Y}+2}{2}\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=\binom{i^{\prime}+1}{2}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

so
\Delta(\tau_{(j,0)})=2\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)})
as required.

Otherwise, if
h_{1}>1

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(j,0)})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})(i-(i-1)+1)\binom{(i-1)-\mathcal{Y}+\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{X}}\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=2\cdot\binom{i^{\prime}-1+\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{X}}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\binom{(i-1)-\mathcal{Y}+\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{X}}\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})=\binom{i^{\prime}-1+\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{X}}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega}).

The case when
|\mathcal{T}_{i,j}|=3

We have two cases to consider:

i)

\mathcal{T}_{i,j}=\{\tau_{(j,0)},\tau_{(j,-1)},\tau_{(j,1)}\}
, and

ii)

\mathcal{T}_{i,j}=\{\tau_{(j,0)},\tau_{(j,-1)},\tau^{(j,1)}\}
.

Case i)

Here
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}=\{\tau_{(j,0)},\tau_{(j,-1)},\tau_{(j,1)}\}
.
Once again, we must have that
\tau_{(j,0)}=\tau_{1}
, so

\displaystyle\tau_{(j,0)}

\displaystyle=\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},\underbrace{i}_{j\text{th position}},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1},h_{1}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big)

\displaystyle\tau_{(j,-1)}

\displaystyle=\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},\underbrace{i-1}_{j\text{th position}},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1},h_{1}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big)

\displaystyle\tau_{(j,1)}

\displaystyle=\big(\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}^{G_{1},H_{1}}\quad},\underbrace{i-2}_{j\text{th position}},\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{1},h_{1}}\quad},\dots,\boxed{\quad\mathcal{I}_{g_{\omega},h_{\omega}}\quad}\big)

where
G_{1}\geq 1
and
g_{1}\geq 2
.
Since
\tau_{(j,v+1)}\not\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
for all
1\leq v\leq\omega
, there are three possibilities.

1)

If
g_{v}\geq\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}+1
for all
1\leq v\leq\omega
, then
\mathcal{R}_{2}=\{j,\tau_{j}-1\}
, that is there are more blue nodes below row
j
(see
4.10
) and
\mathcal{Y}=0
for
\mathfrak{d}(i)
in each
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
.
This case also includes
j=R
.

2)

If
h_{1}\geq g_{1}
, then row
j+h_{1}
already crosses the blue line and
\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})
is the same in all three partitions in
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
.

3)

If for some
1\leq v^{\prime}<v\leq\omega
we have that
g_{v^{\prime}}>\sum_{\rho=1}^{v^{\prime}}h_{\rho}+1
and
g_{v}\leq\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}
, we see that
\mathfrak{d}(g_{1}^{v^{\prime}})
is the same in all partitions in
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
and we compute
\mathfrak{d}(g_{v})
for each.
Here, if
g_{v}<\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}
, then
\mathcal{Y}=i-g_{v}
in
\mathfrak{d}(i)
for all three partitions above, however if
g_{v}=\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}
, then
\mathcal{Y}
is different in
\mathfrak{d}(i)\in\Delta(\tau_{(j,0)})
from
\mathcal{Y}
in
\mathfrak{d}(i)
in the other two.

Thus, we have three computations: case 2) breaks into
g_{1}=h_{1}
and
h_{1}>g_{1}
(if
g_{1}\geq h_{1}+1
, then the computation is identical to the
h_{1}>g_{1}
case by setting
\mathcal{Y}=0
in
\mathfrak{d}(i)
) and the special case from case 3).

Case 2a):
If
h_{1}=g_{1}
, we have that

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(j,0)})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})(i^{\prime}+1)\binom{(i-g_{1})+h_{1}-\mathcal{Y}}{h_{1}}\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=(i^{\prime}+1)\binom{i^{\prime}}{h_{1}}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

and

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})((i-1)-(i-g_{1})+1)\binom{(i-g_{1})+h_{1}+1-\mathcal{Y}}{h_{1}+1}\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=g_{1}\cdot\binom{i^{\prime}+1}{h_{1}+1}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

and

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(j,1)})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})((i-2)-(i-g_{1})+1)\binom{(i-g_{1})+h_{1}+1-\mathcal{Y}}{h_{1}+1}\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=(g_{1}-1)\cdot\binom{i^{\prime}+1}{h_{1}+1}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

Now

(i^{\prime}+1)\binom{i^{\prime}}{h_{1}}=\frac{(i^{\prime}+1)!}{h_{1}!(i^{\prime}-h_{1})!},

so

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(j,0)})+\Delta(\tau_{(j,1)})=

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{(i^{\prime}+1)!}{h_{1}!(i^{\prime}-h_{1})!}+(h_{1}-1)\frac{(i^{\prime}+1)!}{(h_{1}+1)!(i^{\prime}-h_{1})!}\Big)\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=2h_{1}\cdot\frac{(i^{\prime}+1)!}{(h_{1}+1)!(i^{\prime}-h_{1})!}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})=2\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)})

as required.

Case 2b):
Otherwise, if
h_{1}>g_{1}
,

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(j,0)})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})(i-(i-g_{1})+1)\mathfrak{d}(g_{1}^{\omega})=(g_{1}+1)\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{1}^{\omega})

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})(i-1-(i-g_{1})+1)\mathfrak{d}(g_{1}^{\omega})=g_{1}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{1}^{\omega})

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(j,1)})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})(i-2-(i-g_{1})+1)\mathfrak{d}(g_{1}^{\omega})=(g_{1}-1)\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{1}^{\omega}).

Hence
\Delta(\tau_{(j,0)})+\Delta(\tau_{(j,1)})=2\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)})
as required.
We see that the final computation also applies to the case when
j=R
, as then
i-g_{1}=0
and
\mathfrak{d}(g_{1}^{\omega})
is empty.

Case 3):
If
g_{v^{\prime}}>\sum_{\rho=1}^{v^{\prime}}h_{\rho}+1
for all
1\leq v^{\prime}<v\leq\omega
and
g_{v}=\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}
.

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(j,0)})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})(i-\mathcal{Y}+1)\mathfrak{d}(g_{1}^{v-1})\binom{i-g_{v}-\mathcal{Y}+\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}}{\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}}\mathfrak{d}(g_{v+1}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=(i^{\prime}+1)\cdot\binom{i^{\prime}}{g_{v}}\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{1}^{v-1})\mathfrak{d}(g_{v+1}^{\omega})

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})((i-1)-(i-g_{v})+1)\mathfrak{d}(g_{1}^{v-1})\binom{i-g_{v}-\mathcal{Y}+\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}+1}{\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}+1}\mathfrak{d}(g_{v+1}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=g_{v}\cdot\binom{i^{\prime}+1}{g_{v}+1}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{1}^{v-1})\mathfrak{d}(g_{v+1}^{\omega})

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(j,1)})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})((i-2)-(i-g_{v})+1)\mathfrak{d}(g_{1}^{v-1})\binom{i-g_{v}-\mathcal{Y}+\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}+1}{\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}+1}\mathfrak{d}(g_{v+1}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=(g_{v}-1)\cdot\binom{i^{\prime}+1}{g_{v}+1}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{1}^{v-1})\mathfrak{d}(g_{v+1}^{\omega})

Comparing coefficients with case 2a), we see that
\Delta(\tau_{(j,0)})+\Delta(\tau_{(j,1)})=2\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)})
.

Case ii)

Here
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}=\{\tau_{(j,0)},\tau_{(j,-1)},\tau^{(j,1)}\}
.
Similar to the previous case, however we have that
g_{1}=1
and
G_{2}\neq 1
.
As
G_{s+1}\neq\sum_{\rho=1}^{s}H_{s}+1
for all
1\leq s\leq\alpha-1
, we only have two cases to consider:
h_{1}=1
or
h_{1}>1
.
(We do not have case 3 as
g_{1}\geq 1
.)

We consider the case when
G_{1}=0
below.
However it is easy to see that if
G_{1}\geq 2
, then setting
\mathcal{X}_{i}=2
and replacing
\mathfrak{d}(G_{2}^{\alpha})
with
\mathfrak{d}(G_{1}^{\alpha})
yields the same result.
In general,
\mathcal{X}_{i}=\sum_{\rho=1}^{y}H_{\rho}
where
y
is minimal such that
G_{y+1}>\sum_{\rho=1}^{y}H_{\rho}+1
.

Case 1:
If
h_{1}=1
, then

\displaystyle\tau_{(j,0)}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{2}^{\alpha})\binom{i^{\prime}+\mathcal{X}_{i}}{\mathcal{X}_{i}}\cdot(i-1-\mathcal{Y}+1)\cdot\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=\binom{i^{\prime}+\mathcal{X}_{i}}{\mathcal{X}_{i}}\cdot i^{\prime}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{2}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle\tau_{(j,-1)}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{2}^{\alpha})\binom{i-(i-1)+\mathcal{X}_{i}-1}{\mathcal{X}_{i}-1}\binom{i-1-\mathcal{Y}+\mathcal{X}_{i}+1}{\mathcal{X}_{i}+1}\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}_{i}\cdot\binom{i^{\prime}+\mathcal{X}_{i}}{\mathcal{X}_{i}+1}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{2}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle\tau^{(j,1)}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{2}^{\alpha})\binom{i-(i-1)+\mathcal{X}_{i}-2}{\mathcal{X}_{i}-2}\binom{i-1-\mathcal{Y}+\mathcal{X}_{i}+1}{\mathcal{X}_{i}+1}\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{X}_{i}-1)\cdot\binom{i^{\prime}+\mathcal{X}_{i}}{\mathcal{X}_{i}+1}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{2}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega}).

We also see that

\binom{i^{\prime}+\mathcal{X}_{i}}{\mathcal{X}_{i}}=\frac{(i^{\prime}+\mathcal{X}_{i})!}{\mathcal{X}_{i}!\cdot i^{\prime}!}=\frac{\mathcal{X}_{i}+1}{i^{\prime}}\cdot\binom{i^{\prime}+\mathcal{X}_{i}}{\mathcal{X}_{i}+1}.

Thus

\Delta(\tau_{(j,0)})+\Delta(\tau^{(j,1)})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{X}_{i}}\cdot\Big((\mathcal{X}_{i}+1)+(\mathcal{X}_{i}-1)\Big)\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)})=2\cdot\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)}).

Case 2:
If
h_{1}>1
, then

\displaystyle\tau_{(j,0)}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{2}^{\alpha})\binom{i-(i-1)+\mathcal{X}_{i}}{\mathcal{X}_{i}}\binom{i-1-\mathcal{Y}+\mathcal{X}_{i-1}}{\mathcal{X}_{i-1}}\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{X}_{i}+1)\cdot\binom{i^{\prime}-1+\mathcal{X}_{i-1}}{\mathcal{X}_{i-1}}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{2}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle\tau_{(j,-1)}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{2}^{\alpha})\binom{i-(i-1)+\mathcal{X}_{i}-1}{\mathcal{X}_{i}-1}\binom{i-1-\mathcal{Y}+\mathcal{X}_{i-1}}{\mathcal{X}_{i-1}}\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}_{i}\cdot\binom{i^{\prime}-1+\mathcal{X}_{i-1}}{\mathcal{X}_{i-1}}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{2}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle\tau^{(j,1)}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}(G_{2}^{\alpha})\binom{i-(i-1)+\mathcal{X}_{i}-2}{\mathcal{X}_{i}-2}\binom{i-1-\mathcal{Y}+\mathcal{X}_{i-1}}{\mathcal{X}_{i-1}}\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{X}_{i}-1)\cdot\binom{i^{\prime}-1+\mathcal{X}_{i-1}}{\mathcal{X}_{i-1}}\cdot\mathfrak{d}(G_{2}^{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(g_{2}^{\omega}).

The case when
|\mathcal{T}_{i,j}|\geq 4

We have four cases to consider:

i)

\mathcal{T}_{i,j}=\{\tau_{(j,0)},\tau_{(j,-1)},\tau_{(j,1)},\tau^{(j,1)}\}
,

ii)

\mathcal{T}_{i,j}=\{\tau_{(j,0)},\tau_{(j,-1)},\tau_{(j,1)},\dots,\tau_{(j,z+1)}\}
,

iii)

\mathcal{T}_{i,j}=\{\tau_{(j,0)},\tau_{(j,-1)},\tau^{(j,1)},\dots,\tau^{(j,x+1)}\}
, and

iv)

\mathcal{T}_{i,j}=\{\tau_{(j,0)},\tau_{(j,-1)},\tau_{(j,1)},\dots,\tau_{(j,z+1)},\tau^{(j,1)},\dots,\tau^{(j,x+1)}\}
.

This case can be considered similarly.
Details can be included by switching the toggle in the
arXiv
version of this paper.
∎

Example 4.12
.

Continuing with
4.10
, we have that

\Delta(\tau_{(4,-1)})=\Delta(10,8,7,6,5,3^{2},2^{3})=\frac{12\cdot 11\cdot 9\cdot 7\cdot 6\cdot 5\cdot 4\cdot 3}{2}=\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)}).

Then

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(4,0)})

\displaystyle=\Delta(10,8,7^{2},5,3^{2},2^{3})=\frac{8}{3\cdot 5}\cdot\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)}),

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(4,1)})

\displaystyle=\Delta(10,8,7,5^{2},3^{2},2^{3})=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)}),

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(4,2)})

\displaystyle=\Delta(10,8,7,4^{2},3^{2},2^{3})=\frac{2}{2\cdot 4}\cdot\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)}),

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau_{(4,3)})

\displaystyle=\Delta(10,8,7,4,2^{6})=\frac{1}{4\cdot 5}\cdot\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)}),

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau^{(4,1)})

\displaystyle=\Delta(10,8,6^{2},5,3^{2},2^{3})=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)}),

\displaystyle\Delta(\tau^{(4,2)})

\displaystyle=\Delta(10,6^{3},5,3^{2},2^{3})=\frac{1}{2\cdot 3}\cdot\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)}).

So we have

\sum_{\rho=0}^{3}\Delta(\tau_{(4,\rho)})+\sum_{\rho=1}^{2}\Delta(\tau^{(4,\rho)})=2\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)})

as required.

At this point, we turn our attention back to tableaux of hook shapes and give a very useful way to label (some of) them.

Definition 4.13
.

Let
\lambda=(a,1^{b})
and
\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{P}(\lambda)
.
Fix a set of positive even integers
\mathcal{E}=\{e_{1},\dots,e_{\rho}\}
such that
e_{i}+2=e_{i+1}
for all
1\leq i\leq\rho-1
and
1\leq\rho\leq a-1
.
We define
\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}\subset\mathsf{P}(\lambda)
to be the set of all
\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{P}(\lambda)
, such that for some
v_{i}<v_{i+1}
,
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
contains entries
\mathtt{p}_{r_{1}},\mathtt{p}_{r_{2}},\dots,\mathtt{p}_{r_{s}},\mathcal{E},\mathtt{p}_{r_{s+1}}\mathtt{p}_{r_{s+2}},\dots,\mathtt{p}_{r_{d}},(n)
in this order where
2d+\rho+1(+1)=a
(we see that
n\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
can only happen if
n
is even).
If
\mathtt{T}_{\underline{0}}
is the
‘least dominant’
pair-tableau in
\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}
, that is
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T}_{\underline{0}})
contains entries
\mathtt{p}_{e_{1}-2x-1},\mathtt{p}_{e_{1}-2x+1},\dots,\mathtt{p}_{e_{1}-3},\mathtt{p}_{e_{1}-1},\mathcal{E},\newline
\mathtt{p}_{e_{\rho}+3},\mathtt{p}_{e_{\rho}+5},\dots,\mathtt{p}_{n-2},\mathtt{p}_{n}
(or shift each entry by one if
n
is even) for some
1<x
such that
e_{1}-2x-1\geq 3
, we define
\mathtt{T}_{(1)}:=\mathtt{T}_{\underline{0}}
with corresponding
Z_{1}=(0,0,\dots,0)
of length
d
.

Otherwise, we label each
\mathtt{T}_{(i)}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}
by
Z_{i}=(\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\dots,\delta_{d})
where

\delta_{j}=\begin{cases}\frac{1(j)-i(j)}{2}-\rho\quad\qquad\text{if }1(j)>e_{\rho}\text{ and }i(j)<e_{1}\\[10.00002pt]
\frac{1(j)-i(j)}{2}\quad\qquad\qquad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and
\mathtt{p}_{1(j)}
(resp.
\mathtt{p}_{i(j)}
) is the
j
-th pair in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T}_{(1)})
(resp.
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T}_{(i)})
) and
1\leq j\leq d
.
We see that
0\leq\delta_{j}\leq(b-\rho)/2
.

In particular, for
\mathtt{T}_{(i)},\mathtt{T}_{(j)}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}
, we have that
i<j
if
Z_{i}<Z_{j}
in the lexicographic order on partitions.

Example 4.14
.

Let
\lambda=(5,1^{4})
and
\mathcal{E}=\varnothing
.
Then there are six tableaux in
\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}
.

Indeed, we have
Z_{1}=(0,0)<Z_{2}=(1,0)<Z_{3}=(1,1)<Z_{4}=(2,0)<Z_{5}=(2,1)<Z_{6}=(2,2)
.

Remark
.

Hence, now we have a way to label certain hook tableaux by partitions.
In fact, we will see later that
\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{Z})
uniquely determines the set

\mathcal{E}=\{\mathtt{T}^{\mu}(m,\mu_{m})\text{ is even}\mid 1\leq m\leq Z\},

and by
3.30
and
4.1
if
\varphi(z^{\mu})=v=\sum c_{\mathtt{T}}v_{\mathtt{T}}\in S^{\lambda}
, then
c_{\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
only if
\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}
.

If
Z_{p}=\tau_{(j,y)}
or
\tau^{(j,y)}
labels
\mathtt{T}_{(p)}
for some fixed
i
, we write
Z_{p}\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
and label the corresponding basis vector by
v_{\tau({j,y})}
.
We will always make sure that it is clear from the context whether we are referring to
\tau_{(j,y)}
or
\tau^{(j,y)}
.
To simplify notation, we also write
v_{s_{k}\tau(j,y)}
for the basis vector corresponding to the tableau
s_{k}\mathtt{T}_{(p)}
.

For
k
odd such that
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
, we will rename the different actions appearing in
3.30
depending on the number of pairs involved in the resulting rotation (see
3.21
and
3.28
).

•

0-0
move:
\mathtt{p}_{k}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
.

•

1-1
move:
\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
\mathtt{p}_{k}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
.

•

(L)-(L{-}1)
move:
\mathtt{p}_{k},\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
.
Here
\mathtt{T}
is as in
3.19
and
L=m+1
.
In general, we will refer to these as
leg moves
.

•

(A{-}1)-(A)
move:
\mathtt{p}_{k},\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
.
Here
\mathtt{T}
is as in
3.26
and
A=m+1
.
In general, we will refer to these as
arm moves
.

Abusing notation, if
\tau_{(j,y)}
or
\tau^{(j,y)}
labels
\mathtt{T}_{(p)}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}
, we write
\operatorname{Arm}(\tau(j,y))
and
\operatorname{Leg}(\tau(j,y))
for
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T}_{(p)})
and
\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T}_{(p)})
, respectively.
For the rest of this section, we also assume that
\mathtt{p}_{k}
is the
j
th pair in
\operatorname{Arm}(\tau(j,0))
and simply write
\tau(y)
instead of
\tau_{(j,y)}
and
\tau^{(j,y)}
.

By
3.30
, we see that if
\psi_{k}\neq 0
, then the pair
\mathtt{p}_{k}
is rotated into
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
.
Assume that
\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})\neq 0
and
\varphi(z^{\mu})=v\sum c_{\mathtt{T}}v_{\mathtt{T}}
.
When considering the action of
\psi_{k}
on
v
, we would like to break
v
into smaller sums such that

(4.1)

\psi_{k}v=\psi_{k}\sum_{x}c_{\mathtt{T}_{(x)}}v_{\mathtt{T}_{(x)}}=\sum_{x}\sum_{y}c_{\mathtt{T}_{(y)}}\underbrace{\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}_{(y)}}}_{=a_{y}\cdot v_{s_{k}\mathtt{T}_{(x)}}}.

(By
3.30
, we already know that
a=1
or
-2
.)
The rest of this section is dedicated to finding the right set of tableaux and coefficients and proving that

\psi_{k}\sum_{y}c_{\mathtt{T}_{(y)}}v_{\mathtt{T}_{(y)}}=0.

Lemma 4.15
.

If
\mathtt{T}_{(p)}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}
with
Z_{p}\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
and
\mathtt{p}_{k}
is the
j
th pair in
\operatorname{Arm}(\tau(0))
and
e_{1}\neq k+1
, then

i)

\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}_{(p)}}=a_{\tau}\cdot v_{s_{k}\tau_{(0)}}
for all odd
3\leq k\leq n-2
where
a_{\tau}=-2
if
Z_{p}=\tau_{(-1)}
and
a_{\tau}=1
otherwise,

ii)

if
k=n-1
is odd, then

\psi_{k}v_{{\mathtt{T}_{(p)}}}=\begin{cases}v_{s_{k}\mathtt{T}_{(p)}}&\text{if }n\in\operatorname{Leg}(\tau(0))\\
0&\text{if }n\in\operatorname{Arm}(\tau(0)),\end{cases}

iii)

\psi_{r}v_{{\mathtt{T}_{(p)}}}=v_{s_{r}\mathtt{T}_{(p)}}
if
r\in\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

Case iii) follows from
4.6
.
If
k=n-1
and
n\in\operatorname{Arm}(\tau(0))
, then
\psi_{n-1}v_{{\mathtt{T}_{(p)}}}=0
by
3.30
as
n\in\operatorname{Arm}(\tau(v))
for all
\mathtt{T}_{(p)}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}
.
If
k=n-1
and
n\in\operatorname{Leg}(\tau(0))
, the statement holds by
3.30
.

For case i), we first consider leg moves, that is for
v_{\tau({y})}
the corresponding
\mathtt{T}_{(p)}
is labelled by
Z_{p}=\tau_{(y)}
.
Since
e_{1}\neq k+1
, it is easy to see that for all
3\leq k\leq n-2
,

\displaystyle\psi_{k}v_{\tau({0})}

\displaystyle=v_{s_{k}\tau(0)}\,\qquad\qquad\text{as }\mathtt{p}_{k}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\tau(0)),\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\tau(0))

\displaystyle\psi_{k}v_{\tau({-1})}

\displaystyle=-2v_{s_{k}\tau(0)}\qquad\;\;\;\text{as }\mathtt{p}_{k}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\tau(-1)),\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\tau(-1))

Also, by
3.30

\psi_{k}v_{\tau({1})}=v_{s_{k}\tau(0)}

as
\mathtt{p}_{k},\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\tau(1))
and
\mathtt{p}_{x}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\tau(1))
where
x\geq k+4
(
\mathtt{p}_{x}
must exist by definition).
The condition
e_{1}\geq k+3
or
e_{1}\leq k-3
ensures that for all
1\leq v\leq z
we have that
\mathtt{p}_{k},\mathtt{p}_{k+2},\mathtt{p}_{x}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\tau(v+1))
(as
g_{1}\geq 2
).
Further, the position of
\mathcal{E}
does not actually matter, since by
3.30
if
\mathtt{p}_{k},\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\tau(v+1))
, to compute
\psi_{k}v_{\tau({v+1})}
we only need to count the pairs in
\operatorname{Leg}(\tau(v+1))
and
\operatorname{Arm}(\tau(v+1))
, starting with
\mathtt{p}_{k}
and even entries are ignored.
After
\mathtt{p}_{k}
, we see that there are
g_{1}
pairs in
\operatorname{Leg}(\tau(v+1))
which are smaller than the one in position
j
in
\operatorname{Arm}(\tau(v+1))
.
The next
h_{1}
pairs are in
\operatorname{Arm}(\tau(v+1))
, followed by
g_{2}-g_{1}
pairs in the leg.
In general, each
g_{m}
(
1\leq m\leq v
) many pairs in
\operatorname{Leg}(\tau(v+1))
are followed by
g_{m+1}-g_{m}
pairs in
\operatorname{Arm}(\tau(v+1))
.
In total there are
\sum_{1}^{v}h_{m}+1=g_{v}
pairs in
\operatorname{Arm}(\tau(v+1))
between positions
j
and
j+g_{v+1}-1
.
Then, we count the number of pairs in
\operatorname{Leg}(\tau(v+1))
as

g_{1}+g_{2}-g_{1}+g_{3}-g_{2}+\dots+g_{v}-g_{v-1}+1=g_{v}+1

where the plus one accounts for the pair
\mathtt{p}_{k}
.

Assume that
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
.
Using the facts that for all
1\leq r\leq\sum_{\rho=1}^{v}h_{\rho}
,
\tau_{j+r}\leq\tau_{j}-1-r
, and that for all
\sum_{\rho=1}^{w-1}h_{\rho}<r\leq\sum_{\rho=1}^{w}h_{\rho}
with
1\leq w\leq v
, we have that
\tau_{j+r}=i-g_{w}
, we see that

\displaystyle i-g_{w}=\tau_{j+r}

\displaystyle\leq i-1-\sum^{w}_{\rho=1}h_{\rho}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow g_{w}\geq\sum_{\rho=1}^{w}h_{\rho}+1

In particular, if
g_{w}=\sum_{\rho=1}^{w}h_{\rho}+1
, then
\tau_{(j,w+1)}\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
.
Hence
g_{v}
is the minimal number of pairs in
\operatorname{Arm}(\tau(v+1))
in the sense of
3.19
.
Thus all
\mathtt{T}_{(p)}
labelled by
Z_{p}\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
satisfy the conditions of case 3 in
3.30
, so
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}_{(p)}}=v_{s_{k}\tau(0)}
as claimed.

Arm moves can be proved in a similar way.
∎

Using the previous lemma, we will show that setting
c_{\mathtt{T}}=\Delta(\mathtt{T})
and
\mathtt{T}_{(y)}\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
in
eq.

4.1
, we obtain a homomorphism given by

\psi_{k}v=\psi_{k}\sum_{\mathtt{T}_{(x)}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}}c_{\mathtt{T}_{(x)}}v_{\mathtt{T}_{(x)}}=\psi_{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq R\\
0\leq i\leq\frac{b-\rho}{2}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}}\Delta(\tau)v_{\tau}}_{=:\mathcal{S}_{i,j}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq R\\
0\leq i\leq\frac{b-\rho}{2}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}}\Delta(\tau)\underbrace{\psi_{k}v_{\tau}}_{=a_{\tau}v_{s_{k}\tau(0)}}

where
\Delta(\tau)
is computed according to
4.7
.

Remark
.

If
\mathcal{S}_{i,j}
is as above and
|\mathcal{T}_{i,j}|=m
, then it is easy to see that
2\leq m\leq R+2
.
The lower bound is clear, as by definition we have that
\{\tau_{(j,0)},\tau_{(j,-1)}\}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
.
For the upper bound, we just need to count the number of possible leg and arm moves, which gives us
R-j+1+j-1+2=R+2
where the plus two accounts for the moves
0-0
and
1-1
.

Proposition 4.16
.

Suppose that
\mathtt{T}_{(p)}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}
with corresponding
Z_{p}
and that
3\leq k\leq n-2
is odd such that
\mathtt{p}_{k}
is the
j
th (where
1\leq j\leq R
) pair in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T}_{(p)})
and
\delta_{j}=i
(where
0\leq i\leq\frac{b-\rho}{2}
) in
Z_{p}
.
Then for each pair of integers
(i,j)
, we have that
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}_{(p)}}=v_{s_{k}\tau(0)}
if and only if
e_{1}\neq k+1
and
Z_{p}\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
.

Proof.

Fix
3\leq k\leq n-2
.
We have already shown in
4.15
that if
Z_{p}\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
and
e_{1}\neq k+1
, then
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}_{(p)}}=c\cdot v_{\tau({0})}
where
c=-2
if
Z_{p}=\tau_{(-1)}
and
c=1
otherwise.

To prove the converse, suppose
e_{1}=k+1
.
In this case,

\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0

for all
\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}
by
3.30
since we either have
k-1,k,k+1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T}),k+2\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
or
k+1\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T}),k-1,k,k+2\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
.
For the rest of the proof, we assume that
e_{1}\neq k+1
.

By
3.30
, we see that

\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}_{(p)}}=-2v_{s_{k-1}s_{k+1}s_{k}\mathtt{T}_{(p)}}

if and only if
\mathtt{p}_{k+2}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
\mathtt{p}_{k}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
which is precisely the case when
Z_{p}=\tau(-1)
(then we also see that
s_{k-1}s_{k+1}s_{k}\mathtt{T}_{(p)}=s_{k}\tau(0)
).
Since
e_{1}\neq k+1
,
\mathtt{p}_{k+2}
exists for all
3\leq k\leq n-2
.
We also see that if
k=n-1
, then
j=R
and
i=0
, so
\tau(-1)
is not a valid partition and
\mathcal{T}_{i,j}=\varnothing
.

Assume that
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}_{(p)}}\neq 0
such that the resulting rotation changes at most one pair in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T}_{(p)})
.
It is clear that there are at most three possible moves (
0-0,1-1,2-1
) and we must have that
\delta_{j-1}\geq i+1
(as otherwise the
1-2
move can occur) and
\delta_{j+1}=i-1
or
\delta_{j+1}\leq i-2
such that if
\delta_{j+1}=i-x
, then it is repeated either less than or more than
x
times.
We see that any such
\mathtt{T}_{(p)}
is labelled by some
Z_{p}\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
.

Next, assume that
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}_{(p)}}\neq 0
such that the resulting rotation changes at most two pairs in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T}_{(p)})
(these moves are three listed in the previous paragraph plus
3-2
and
1-2
).

•

If
\delta_{j-1}=\delta_{j}=\delta_{j+1}=i
, then
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}_{(p)}}
is either zero or the resulting rotation moves
\mathtt{p}_{k}
from the
j
th position.

•

If
\delta_{j+1}=i-1
, then the
0-0
move is allowed by.
But by
3.30
, we see that leg moves are only possible if
\mathtt{p}_{k}
and
\mathtt{p}_{k+2}
are both in the leg and pair in position
j
is larger than
k+2
, which requires
\delta_{j+1}\leq i-2
.
If
\delta_{j-1}\geq i+1
, then we already saw that the rotation can involve at most one pair in the arm.
If
\delta_{j-1}=i
, we must have that
\delta_{j-2}\geq i+1
, otherwise the
2-3
move would be allowed.

•

If
\delta_{j-1}=i=\delta_{j}
, and
\delta_{j+1}\leq i-x
(
x\geq 2
), then
x
must be as above and
\delta_{j-2}\geq i+2
.
If
\delta_{j-1}=i+1
, we already know that the rotation can change at most one pair in the arm.

We see that each
\mathtt{T}_{(p)}
described above is labelled by
Z_{p}\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
.

Next, assume that
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}_{(p)}}\neq 0
such that the resulting rotation changes at least three pairs in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T}_{(p)})
and that
\tau_{(j,0)}
is of the form as in
4.9
.

Suppose that for some
1\leq v^{\prime}\leq\omega
such that
\tau_{(j,v^{\prime})}\not\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}
.
It is clear that if
g_{v^{\prime}}\neq\sum_{\rho=1}^{v^{\prime}}h_{\rho}+1
, then by
3.30
and a similar argument as in the proof of
4.15
, we have that
\psi_{k}v_{\tau({v^{\prime}})}=0
.

Finally, suppose that
g_{v^{\prime}}=\sum_{\rho=1}^{v^{\prime}}h_{\rho}+1
and for some
1\leq r\leq\sum^{v^{\prime}}_{\rho=1}h_{\rho}
, we have that
\tau_{j+r}>\tau_{j}-2-r
.
This implies that
g_{w}<\sum_{\rho=1}^{w}h_{\rho}+1
for some
w\leq v^{\prime}
.
In particular, looking at the proof of
4.15
, the minimal number of pairs is not
g_{v^{\prime}}
in this case, hence
\psi_{k}v_{\tau({v^{\prime}})}\neq v_{s_{k}\tau(0)}
.

Arm moves can be considered using a similar reasoning.
∎

Corollary 4.17
.

We have that
\psi_{k}\mathcal{S}_{i,j}=0
for all odd
3\leq k\leq n-2,1\leq j\leq R
and
0\leq i\leq\frac{b-\rho}{2}
.

Proof.

By
4.11
and
4.15
, we have that

\displaystyle\psi_{k}\mathcal{S}_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{i,j}}\Delta(\tau)v_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}\big(\sum_{v=0}^{z+1}\Delta(\tau_{(j,v)})v_{\tau({v})}+\sum_{s=1}^{x+1}\Delta(\tau^{(j,s)})v_{\tau({s})}\big)+\psi_{k}\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)})v_{\tau({-1})}

\displaystyle=2\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)})v_{s_{k}\tau(0)}+(-2)\Delta(\tau_{(j,-1)})v_{s_{k}\tau(0)}=0.\qed

Proposition 4.18
.

If
\lambda=(a,1^{b})
with
a\geq 3
and
b\geq 2
, then every
\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}
appears in at least one
\mathcal{S}_{i,j}
for some odd
3\leq k\leq n-2
.

Proof.

We see that
\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}
does not appear in
\mathcal{S}_{i,j}
if and only if
\psi_{k}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
for all odd
3\leq k\leq n-2
, but this is impossible.
∎

4.2.
Homomorphisms from hook partitions

In this section, we study homomorphisms from
S^{\mu}
to
S^{\lambda}
where
\mu
is also a hook shape.
First, we look at
\mu=(1^{n})
or
(n)
and completely determine
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
in these cases.
Next, we generalise these results for all
\mu=(c,1^{d})
by finding at least one nonzero homomorphism if it exists.
The results are summarised in
4.32
.

Remark
.

As we will see, all the homomorphisms constructed in
sections

4.2
and
4.3
have integer coefficients and leading coefficient one, so our results hold for any field
\mathbb{F}
with
\Ch(\mathbb{F})=p
, we only need to reduce the coefficients modulo
p
.

4.2.1.
\mu=(1^{n})
or
\mu=(n)

It is clear that if
\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
then
\varphi(z^{\mu})=v=\sum c_{\mathtt{T}}v_{\mathtt{T}}
such that
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}
.
This together with
4.2
and
4.3
imply that

\varphi(z^{\mu})=v=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\\
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}\end{subarray}}c_{\mathtt{T}}v_{\mathtt{T}}.

By
4.6
, we see that
(J^{\mu}_{1}+J^{\mu}_{2})v=0
for all such
v
.
If
\mu=(n)
, there are no Garnir nodes and if
\mu=(1^{n})
, the Garnir relations are given by
\psi_{k}z^{\mu}=0
for all
1\leq k\leq n-1
.
Hence in order to prove the existence of a homomorphism, we only need to show that
\psi_{k}v=0
for all
2\leq k\leq n-1
and this will ensure that
(J^{\mu}_{3}+J^{\mu}_{4})v=0
.
The residue conditions also imply that all entries in
\mathtt{T}
must appear in pairs (except if
n
is even, in which case
n
is not in a pair) and so
\psi_{r}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
for all even
2\leq r\leq n-2
.

Remark
.

Considering
4.2
and
4.3
, it might seem that we could still have
r-1,r+2\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
r,r+1\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
for some odd
r
such that
\text{\boldmath$i$}_{r-1}=\text{\boldmath$i$}_{r+1}
, however by a similar argument as in the remark after
3.4
, we see that this is impossible.

First, we consider
n
odd.

Proposition 4.19
.

Let
n
be odd,
\mu=(1^{n})
or
(n)
and
\lambda=(a,1^{b})
.
We have that
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is one dimensional, spanned by

\varphi(z^{\mu})=v=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\\
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}\end{subarray}}\Delta(\mathtt{T})v_{\mathtt{T}},

if
a
is odd and zero otherwise.

Proof.

If
n
is odd and
a
is even, then the modules
S^{\mu}
and
S^{\lambda}
lie in different blocks and so
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})=0
.
So we can assume that
a
is odd.
As
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\nu},S^{(1^{n})})
is at most one dimensional, with the only possible homomorphism given by
z^{\nu}\mapsto v_{\mathtt{T}^{(1^{n})}}
, we can use the isomorphism given in the introduction to show that
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is either zero or one.
Thus, once we find a nonzero
v
, then
v
is unique up to scalar multiplication.
By
4.18
, we see that all elements of the set
\{\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\varnothing}\mid\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}\}
appear in
v
and by
4.17
,
\psi_{k}v=0
for odd
2\leq k\leq n-1
, hence
(J^{\mu}_{3}+J^{\mu}_{4})v=0
and we proved the statement.
∎

Example 4.20
.

Let
\mu=(1^{9})
and
\lambda=(5,1^{4})
. Then
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{(1^{9})},S^{(5,1^{4})})=1
and
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}

\vphantom{1}\smash{9}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{8}
\vphantom{1}\smash{9}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}
\vphantom{1}\smash{7}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}
\vphantom{1}\smash{7}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{8}
\vphantom{1}\smash{9}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{8}
\vphantom{1}\smash{9}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}
\vphantom{1}\smash{7}

\vphantom{1}\smash{9}
v=
+2
+3
+3
+6
+6
.

Next we consider
n
even. It is easy to see that by the same reasoning as for
n
odd, we only have to check
(J^{\mu}_{3}+J^{\mu}_{4})v=0
.

Proposition 4.21
.

Let
n
be even,
\mu=(1^{n})
or
(n)
and
\lambda=(a,1^{b})
.
We have that
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})=1
is one dimensional, spanned by

\varphi(z^{\mu})=v=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\\
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}\end{subarray}}\Delta(\mathtt{T})v_{\mathtt{T}},

if
a
is even and zero otherwise.

Proof.

If
a
is even, the proof is essentially the same as in
4.19
.
Assume that there exists some
\varphi^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
with
n
even and
a
odd such that
\varphi^{\prime}(z^{\mu})=v^{\prime}=\sum_{\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{P}(\lambda)}c_{\mathtt{T}}v_{\mathtt{T}}
and
\mathtt{p}_{n-1}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
.
By
3.30
,
\psi_{n-1}v_{\mathtt{T}}=v_{s_{n-1}\mathtt{T}}
.
But it is easy to see that there exists no other
\mathtt{S}\in\mathsf{P}(\lambda)
such that
\psi_{n-1}v_{\mathtt{S}}=v_{s_{n-1}\mathtt{T}}
, hence we must have that
\mathtt{p}_{n-1}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
.

Next, we look at the possible position of
\mathtt{p}_{n-3}
in
\mathtt{T}
:

1)

\mathtt{p}_{n-3}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T}),\mathtt{p}_{n-1}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
: in this case
\psi_{n-3}v_{\mathtt{T}}=v_{s_{n-3}\mathtt{T}}
;

2)

\mathtt{p}_{n-3},\mathtt{p}_{n-1}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
: in this case
\psi_{n-3}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
by
3.30
.

We see that if
n-3\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
, then
\psi_{n-3}v_{\mathtt{T}}\neq 0
by case 1) above.
As
\mathtt{p}_{n-1}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
for all
\mathtt{T}
involved in
v
, it is clear that
\psi_{n-3}v\neq 0
.
Thus only case 2) is possible.
We can use the same reasoning for all
3\leq k<n-3
and conclude that if
n
is even and
a
is odd then
\lambda=(1^{n})
or
(n)
, that is
S^{\mu}\cong S^{\lambda}
and we only have the trivial homomorphism.
∎

Example 4.22
.

Let
\mu=(1^{8})
and
\lambda=(4,1^{4})
. Then
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{(1^{8})},S^{(4,1^{4})})=1
where
\varphi(z^{\mu})=v
and
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}
\vphantom{1}\smash{7}
\vphantom{1}\smash{8}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{8}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}
\vphantom{1}\smash{7}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{8}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}
\vphantom{1}\smash{7}
v=
+2
+3
.

4.2.2.
\mu=(c,1^{d})
and
c>a

The strategy is very similar to that of
section

4.2.1
.
Using
4.2
and
4.3
and the residue conditions, we see that if
(J^{\mu}_{1}+J^{\mu}_{2})v=0
then

v=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\\
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}\end{subarray}}c_{\mathtt{T}}v_{\mathtt{T}}.

Observe that in this case there is one extra Garnir relation
\psi_{G}z^{\mu}=\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\dots\psi_{a}z^{\mu}=0
coming from the Garnir node
\mathtt{T}(1,1)
, but since
\psi_{1}
appears on the left,
\psi_{G}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
for all
\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}
by
3.30
.
Hence to prove that
\varphi(z^{\mu})=v
for
\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})\neq 0
, we only need to show that
\psi_{k}v=0
for all
2\leq k\leq n-1
as this will ensure that
(J^{\mu}_{3}+J^{\mu}_{4})v=0
.
Using
3.30
, we see that the only possible way that some even
r
is unpaired in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
is if
\psi_{r}z^{\mu}\neq 0
.

We need to consider the parities of
n,c
and
a
separately.

Proposition 4.23
.

Let
\mu=(c,1^{d})
and
\lambda=(a,1^{b})
be two partitions of
n
with
c>a
.
Then
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is at least one dimensional if

i)

n
is odd and
c\equiv_{2}a
, or

ii)

n
is even,

and zero otherwise.

Proof.

Define
\bar{\lambda}:=(a,1^{c-a})
.
First, we are going to construct a homomorphism
\bar{\varphi}(z^{(c)})=\bar{v}
with
\bar{v}
summing over elements of the set
\{\bar{\mathtt{T}}\in\mathsf{Z}(\bar{\lambda})_{\mathcal{E}}\mid\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(c)}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\bar{\mathtt{T}}}\}
and then extend this to a homomorphism from
S^{\mu}
to
S^{\lambda}
by placing the entries
c+1,c+2,\dots,n
in
\operatorname{Leg}(\bar{\mathtt{T}})
.

(1)

Odd
n

Just as in
4.19
, we see that if
n
is odd and
c\not\equiv_{2}a
, then
S^{\mu}
and
S^{\lambda}
are in different blocks.
Thus we assume that
c\equiv_{2}a
.

a)

a
odd

In this case
c
is also odd and we take

\bar{v}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{\mathtt{T}}\in\mathsf{Z}(\bar{\lambda})_{\varnothing}\\
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\bar{\mathtt{T}}}\end{subarray}}\Delta(\bar{\mathtt{T}})v_{\bar{\mathtt{T}}}.

b)

a
even

Here
c
is even and we take

\bar{v}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{\mathtt{T}}\in\mathsf{Z}(\bar{\lambda})_{\{c\}}\\
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\bar{\mathtt{T}}}\end{subarray}}\Delta(\bar{\mathtt{T}})v_{\bar{\mathtt{T}}}.

(2)

Even
n

In this case, the residue conditions allow
c
to be either odd or even, depending on where
n
is placed.

a)

a
odd

If
c
is odd, we construct
\bar{v}
as described in case 1.
If
c
is even,
\bar{v}
is the same as in 1b) (note that
n\in\operatorname{Arm}(\bar{\mathtt{T}})
for all
\bar{\mathtt{T}}
appearing in the sum).

b)

a
even

If
c
is odd, then
\bar{v}
agrees with the one in 1a) (observe that
n\in\operatorname{Arm}(\bar{\mathtt{T}})
for all
\bar{\mathtt{T}}
appearing in the sum).
Finally, if
c
is even,
\bar{v}
is the same as in 1b).

We extend each
\bar{\mathtt{T}}
above to
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
by adjoining
c+1,c+2,\dots,n
to
\operatorname{Leg}(\bar{\mathtt{T}})
, giving terms
v
.
By
3.30
, it is clear then that
\psi_{r}v=0=y_{s}v
for all
c<r<n
and
c<s\leq n
and by
4.19
, we have that
J^{\mu}v=0
.
∎

Remark
.

The homomorphisms
\varphi
described in
4.23
might not be unique, indeed if
n
is odd and
c\equiv_{2}a
or
n
is even and
c\not\equiv_{2}a
, then by
4.19
and
4.21
we see that if
\varphi^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{(n)},S^{\lambda})
and
\varphi^{\prime}(z^{(n)})=v^{\prime}
, then there exists some
\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
such that
\varphi(z^{\mu})=v^{\prime}
.
As
c>a
, it is clear that
v\neq v^{\prime}
and we have that
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})\geq 2
.

However, if
n
is even and
c\equiv_{2}a
, then
n\not\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and by a similar reasoning as in
4.21
, we see that the entries
c+1,\dots,n
must all be placed in
\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
.
As
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{(c)},S^{\lambda})=1
, this implies that
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is also one dimensional in this case.

Example 4.24
.

Let
n>7
.
If
\mu=(7,1^{n-7})
and
\lambda=(3,1^{n-3})
, then
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})\geq 2
and the homomorphism described in
4.23
case 1a) is drawn below where we omitted all entries greater than
7
.

v=

4.2.3.
\mu=(c,1^{d})
and
c=a
or
a-1

In
[
Spe14
]
, the author obtained a generalised eigenspace decomposition for
S^{\lambda}
for hook partitions of shape
\lambda
in the case when
n
is odd.
In doing so, he obtained an upper bound on
\dim\End_{R^{\Lambda}_{n}}(S^{\lambda})
and constructed an endomorphism for odd
n
.
Here we summarise these results and extend for the case when
n
is even and
\mu=(a-1,1^{b+1})
.

Proposition 4.25

(
[
Spe15
]
Lemma 3.28 and
[
Spe14
]
, Thm. 4.3)
.

i)

Let
n
and
a
both be odd and take
\lambda=(a,1^{b})
.
Then

\bullet

\dim\End_{R^{\Lambda}_{n}}(S^{\lambda})\leq 1+b/2
if
b<n/2
, and

\bullet

\dim\End_{R^{\Lambda}_{n}}(S^{\lambda})\leq 1+(a-1)/2
if
b\geq n/2
.

ii)

If
n
is even, then
\dim\End_{R^{\Lambda}_{n}}(S^{\lambda})=1
.

Definition 4.26

(cf. Def. 6.1,
[
Spe14
]
)
.

For
n
odd, let
k
and
r
be odd integers with
3\leq k\leq a<r\leq n
.
We will denote by
\mathtt{T}_{k,r}
the tableau with
\{\mathtt{p}_{3},\mathtt{p}_{5},\dots,\mathtt{p}_{a},\mathtt{p}_{r}\}\setminus\{\mathtt{p}_{k}\}
in the arm.
If
n
is even, we choose odd integers
k
and
r
such that
3\leq k\leq a<r\leq n-1
and
\mathtt{T}_{k,r}
denotes the tableau with even arm length where the pairs
\{\mathtt{p}_{3},\mathtt{p}_{5},\dots,\mathtt{p}_{a},\mathtt{p}_{r}\}\setminus\{\mathtt{p}_{k}\}
and
n
are in the arm.

Proposition 4.28

[
Spe14
, cf. Prop. 6.2]
.

If
n
and
a
are both odd and
\mu=\lambda
or if
n
is even and
c=a-1
is odd, then there exists a homomorphism
\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
given by
\varphi(z^{\mu})=v
where

v=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}3\leq k\leq a\\
a+2\leq r\leq n\\
k,r\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{k-1}{2}$}}\cdot\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{n+2-r}{2}$}}v_{\mathtt{T}_{k,r}}.

Proof.

If
n
is odd, the result follows by
[
Spe14
, Prop. 6.2]
.
If
n
is even, by the same reasoning as in the proof of
4.21
, we must have that
n\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T}_{k,r})
.
∎

Example 4.29
.

If
\mu=(7,1^{5})
and
\lambda=(8,1^{4})
, then we have a homomorphism from
S^{\mu}
to
S^{\lambda}
given by

v=v_{\mathtt{T}_{5,9}}+2\;v_{\mathtt{T}_{3,9}}+2\;v_{\mathtt{T}_{5,11}}+4\;v_{\mathtt{T}_{5,9}}+3\;v_{\mathtt{T}_{7,11}}+6\;v_{\mathtt{T}_{7,9}}.

4.2.4.
\mu=(c,1^{d})
and
c\leq a

The next statement follows from
4.19
.

Proposition 4.30
.

If
n
is odd and
c\leq a\leq n
, then
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is at least one dimensional if
c\equiv_{2}a
and zero otherwise.

Proof.

The parity restrictions follow by the same reasoning as in
4.23
.

Assume that
c
and
a
are both odd.
In
4.19
we have already constructed
\varphi(z^{(1^{n})})=v
.
Taking the same
v
here, we already know that
\psi_{k}v=0
for all
2\leq k\leq n-1
, so the condition
\psi_{r}v=0
for
2\leq r\neq c\leq n
is immediately satisfied.

If
c
and
a
are both even, by
4.1
we have that

\varphi(z^{\mu})=v=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\{c\}}\\
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}\end{subarray}}c_{\mathtt{T}}v_{\mathtt{T}}.

Using
4.19
again, we can construct
v
in the same way by setting
c_{\mathtt{T}}=\Delta(\mathtt{T})
.
By
4.16
,
\psi_{c-1}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0
for all
\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\{c\}}
and so by
3.30
and
4.17
,
\psi_{k}v=0
for all
2\leq k\neq c\leq n-1
and we proved the statement.
∎

Proposition 4.31
.

If
n
is even and
c<a
, then
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is at least one dimensional if
c\not\equiv_{2}a
and zero otherwise.

Proof.

If
n
is even and
c\equiv_{2}a
, then we would have

\varphi(z^{\mu})=v=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}\\
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}\end{subarray}}c_{\mathtt{T}}v_{\mathtt{T}}.

By a similar argument as in
4.21
, this is impossible and we must have that
n\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
, but then
c\not\equiv_{2}a
, a contradiction.

If
c
is even and
a
is odd, we see that
\mathcal{E}=\{c\}
and if
c
is odd and
a
is even, then
\mathcal{E}=\varnothing
.
Using a similar reasoning as in the proof of
4.30
, we see that
J^{\mu}v=0
.
∎

We summarise the results from this section in the following statement.

Theorem 4.32
.

Let
\mu=(c,1^{d})
and
\lambda=(a,1^{b})
. Then
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is at least one dimensional if

i)

n
is odd and
c\equiv_{2}a
, or

ii)

n
is even, unless
c\equiv_{2}a
and
c<a
.

Moreover, if
c>a
and
c+n\not\equiv_{2}a
, then
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})\geq 2
.

Remark
.

In particular, if
c=a
is odd and
a\geq 5,b\geq 4
, then
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})\geq 3
, as the non-trivial homomorphisms in
4.28
and
4.30
are clearly distinct.

4.3.
Homomorphisms from non-hooks

In this section, we first use
4.1
to describe homomorphisms from
S^{\mu}
to
S^{\lambda}
where
\mu
is a two-column partition.
Combining this with our findings from the previous sections, we obtain a generalised version of
4.32
which describes
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
in the case when
\mu
is a
two-column hook partition
, a partition of the form
\mu=(c,2^{s},1^{d})
(see
4.36
).

4.3.1.
Homomorphisms from two-column partitions

Theorem 4.33
.

Let
\mu=(2^{r},1^{s})
with
r\geq 1
and
s\geq 0
be a partition of
n
.
Then
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is one dimensional, spanned by

\varphi(z^{\mu})=v=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}\\
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}\\
\mathcal{E}=\{2,4,\dots,2r\}\end{subarray}}\Delta(\mathtt{T})v_{\mathtt{T}},

if
r+1\leq a\leq n-r
, and either

•

n+a\equiv_{2}r
, or

•

n
is even and
a=r+1
,

and zero otherwise.

Proof.

By
4.1
, if
v_{\mathtt{T}}
is involved in
v
and
x=\mathtt{T}^{\mu}(y,2)
, then
x\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
for all
2\leq y\leq r
.
Thus
(J^{\mu}_{1}+J^{\mu}_{2})v=0
if and only if

v=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}\\
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mathtt{T}}\end{subarray}}c_{\mathtt{T}}v_{\mathtt{T}}

where
\mathcal{E}=\{2,4,\dots,2r\}
and so
r+1\leq a\leq n-r
.

Similar to the proof of
4.23
, setting
\bar{\lambda}=(a-r,1^{b-r})
, we can construct

\bar{\varphi}(z^{(s)})=\bar{v}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{\mathtt{T}}\in\mathsf{Z}(\bar{\lambda})_{\varnothing}\\
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\bar{\mathtt{T}}}\end{subarray}}\Delta(\bar{\mathtt{T}})v_{\bar{\mathtt{T}}}.

For each
\bar{\mathtt{T}}
above, we define
\mathtt{T}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
, by increasing every entry in
\bar{\mathtt{T}}
by
2r
and placing the entries in
\mathcal{E}
at the beginning of
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
and
\{3,5,\dots,2r+1\}
at beginning of
\operatorname{Leg}(\mathtt{T})
.
Then we obtain
v
such that
y_{s}v=0
for all
1\leq s\leq n
and
\psi_{k}v=0
for all
k\in\{3,5,\dots,2r-1\}
and
2r+1\leq k\leq n-1
, hence
(J_{1}^{\mu}+J_{2}^{\mu}+J_{3}^{\mu})v=0
.
Next, we look at the Garnir relations.
There are three different non-trivial relations:

1)

G_{1}z^{\mu}=\psi_{k}\psi_{k+1}z^{\mu}=0
if
k=\mathtt{T}^{\mu}(y,1)
for
k
odd and
1\leq y\leq r
,

2)

G_{2}z^{\mu}=\psi_{k+1}\psi_{k}z^{\mu}=0
if
k=\mathtt{T}^{\mu}(y,2)
for
k
even and
1\leq y\leq r
, and

3)

\psi_{k}z^{\mu}=0
for all
2r+1\leq k\leq n-1
.

By
3.30
, we see that
G_{1}v_{\mathtt{T}}=0=G_{2}v_{\mathtt{T}}
and we already proved case 3) above, so
J^{\mu}v=0
.
It is clear that if
n
is odd, then so is
s
.
If
n\equiv_{2}s\not\equiv_{2}(a-r)
, then
S^{(s)}
and
S^{\bar{\lambda}}
lie in different blocks and
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{(s)},S^{\bar{\lambda}})=0
.
Hence if
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})\neq 0
, then
n+(a-r)+r=n+a\equiv_{2}r
.
If
n
is even, using a similar argument as in
4.21
, we must have that
n\in\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
, which implies
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})\neq 0
then
n+a\equiv_{2}r
.
Moreover, if
n
is even, then it is easy to see that there exists
v=v_{\mathtt{T}}
where
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
only contains
\mathcal{E}
, that is
a=r+1
.

Finally, we prove that
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})=1
.
Indeed, if
s\leq 1
, then
\mid\mathtt{T}\in\mathsf{Z}(\lambda)_{\mathcal{E}}\mid=1
and
v=v_{\mathtt{T}}
where
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
contains only entries of
\mathcal{E}
.
If
s>1
, observe that
\bar{\varphi}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{(s)},S^{(a-r,b-r)})
, so
\bar{v}
is unique up to scalar multiplication.
Using a similar argument as in the remark after
4.23
, we see that
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})=1
.
∎

4.3.2.
Homomorphisms from two-column hook partitions

Theorem 4.34
.

Let
\mu=(c,2^{s},1^{d})
with
c
even.
Then
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is at least one dimensional if
a\geq s+2
; and either

•

n
is odd,
a\equiv_{2}s
and
s+2\leq a\leq n-(s+1)
, or

•

n
is even and
s+2\leq a\leq c+s
or
a=c+s+1+2y
for
0\leq y\leq d/2-2
,

and zero otherwise.

Proof.

Using the same method as in
4.33
, it is easy to see that we extend
v
from
4.30
and
4.31
, which is a homomorphism from
S^{(c,1^{d})}
to
S^{(a-s,1^{b-s})}
, to a homomorphism
\varphi(z^{\mu})=v^{\prime}
, where
\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
, by increasing every entry greater than
c+1
by
2s
and placing the entries
\mathcal{E}=\{c+2,c+4,\dots,c+2s\}
in the arm and the entries
\{c+3,c+5,\dots,c+2s+1\}
in the leg.
∎

Example 4.35
.

Let
\mu=(4,2^{2},1^{5})
and
\lambda=(8,1^{5})
.
By
4.30
, there exists
\varphi(z^{(4,1^{5})})=v
as below where
\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{(4,1^{5})},S^{(6,1^{3})})
.
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}

\vphantom{1}\smash{9}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{7}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}
\vphantom{1}\smash{8}
\vphantom{1}\smash{9}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{7}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}
\vphantom{1}\smash{7}
\vphantom{1}\smash{8}
\vphantom{1}\smash{9}
v=
+2
+3

Then we can extend this a homomorphism
v^{\prime}
from
S^{\mu}
to
S^{\lambda}
.
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}
\vphantom{1}\smash{8}
\vphantom{1}\smash{10}
\vphantom{1}\smash{11}
\vphantom{1}\smash{12}
\vphantom{1}\smash{13}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{7}
\vphantom{1}\smash{9}
