\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}
\vphantom{1}\smash{8}
\vphantom{1}\smash{12}
\vphantom{1}\smash{13}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{7}
\vphantom{1}\smash{9}
\vphantom{1}\smash{10}
\vphantom{1}\smash{11}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}
\vphantom{1}\smash{8}
\vphantom{1}\smash{10}
\vphantom{1}\smash{11}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{7}
\vphantom{1}\smash{9}
\vphantom{1}\smash{12}
\vphantom{1}\smash{13}
v^{\prime}=
+2
+3

Combining our results from
4.32
and
4.34
, we have just proved our main theorem:

Theorem 4.36

(Main Theorem)
.

Let
\lambda=(a,1^{b})
and
\mu=(c,2^{s},1^{d})
where if
s>0
then
c
is even.
Then
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is at least one dimensional if for
c
even, we have that
a\geq s+2
and either

•

n
is odd,
a\equiv_{2}s
and
s+1\leq a\leq n-(s+1)
, or

•

n
is even and
s+2\leq a\leq c+s
or
a=c+s+1+2y
for
0\leq y\leq d/2-2
,

and zero otherwise.

Remarks
.

We have a more precise description of the dimensions of
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
in the following cases:

1)

\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is at least three dimensional if
c=a
is odd and
a\geq 5,b\geq 4
,

2)

\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is at least two dimensional if
c>a
(unless we are in case 3b) below),

3)

\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is one dimensional if

a)

n
is even,
c\equiv_{2}a
and
c\geq a
, or

b)

if
c=2
(i.e.
\mu=(2^{s+1},1^{d})
).

If
s=1
and
a=c+1
, then we recover the known Carter-Payne homomorphism of degree
1
and can explicitly describe it by
z^{\mu}\mapsto v_{\mathtt{T}}
, where
\operatorname{Arm}(\mathtt{T})
consists of
2,3,\dots,c,c+2
.
For more details, we refer the reader to
[
LM14
, Thm. 3.12]
.

Remark
.

In this final remark, we give the graded the dimension of the homomorphism from
4.36
.
The following is easy to verify:

1)

If
\mu=(c,1^{d})
, then

\bullet

\deg(\mathtt{T}^{\mu})=(c-1)/2
if
c
is odd, and

\bullet

\deg(\mathtt{T}^{\mu})=c/2
if
c
is even.

2)

If
\mu=(c,2^{s},1^{d})
with
c
even, then
\deg(\mathtt{T}^{\mu})=c/2+s
.

Now we only need to compute
\deg(\mathtt{T})-\deg(\mathtt{T}^{\mu})=:\mathcal{D}
for all cases in
4.36
where
\mathtt{T}
has shape
\lambda=(a,1^{b})
.
Assume that
\mu=(c,1^{d})
.

1)

If
n
is odd, then
\mathcal{D}=(a-c)/2
.

2)

If
n
is even, then

\bullet

\mathcal{D}=(a-c)/2
if
c\equiv_{2}a
,

\bullet

\mathcal{D}=(a-c+1)/2
if
c
is odd and
a
is even, and

\bullet

\mathcal{D}=(a-c+3)/2
if
c
is even and
a
is odd.

Finally, let
\mu=(c,2^{s},1^{d})
with
c
even and
s\geq 1
.

1)

If
n
is odd, then

\bullet

\mathcal{D}=(a-c)/2
if
s
is even, and

\bullet

\mathcal{D}=(a-c+1)/2
if
s
is odd.

2)

If
n
is even, then

\bullet

\mathcal{D}=(a-c+2)/2
if
s
is odd and
a
is even,

\bullet

\mathcal{D}=(a-c+1)/2
if both
s
and
a
are odd,

\bullet

\mathcal{D}=(a-c)/2
if
s
and
a
are both even, and

\bullet

\mathcal{D}=(a-c+3)/2
if
s
is even and
a
is odd.

Then we have that

\dim_{q}\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})=q^{\mathcal{D}},

where
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is as in
4.36
.

4.4.
The trivial module

In the following section, we obtain a graded version of James’s well-known result for the trivial module
S^{(n)}
(see
[
Jam78
, Thm. 24.4]
).
First, we need to recall some elementary results about binomial coefficients which will be used in the statement of
4.38
.
Let
p
be a fixed prime.

Definition 4.37
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we define
\ell_{p}(n)=\min\{i\mid p^{i}>n\}
.
We also set
\ell_{p}(0)=-\infty
.

There are two one dimensional
S^{\lambda}
modules, indexed by the partitions
\lambda=(1^{n})
and
(n)
.
The module
S^{(n)}
is called the
trivial module
and
S^{(1^{n})}
is called the
sign module
.
As
e=2
, we have that
S^{(n)}\cong S^{(1^{n})}
up to grading shift.

Theorem 4.38
.

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots,\mu_{Z})
be a partition of
n
.
Then
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{(1^{n})})
is one dimensional if

i)

\Ch\mathbb{F}=0,\mu_{1}
is odd and
\mu=(\mu_{1},1^{n-\mu_{1}})
or

ii)

\Ch\mathbb{F}=p>0
and

\bullet

for all
1\leq i\leq Z-1
,
\mu_{i}
is odd, and

\bullet

if
n
is odd, then
\mu_{Z}
is odd, and

\bullet

\mu_{i}\equiv-1\;\operatorname{mod}\;2p^{\ell_{p}(a{i+1})}
for all
1\leq i<Z
where
a_{i}=\lfloor\mu_{i}/2\rfloor
,

and zero otherwise.
In this case

\dim_{q}\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})=q^{-(\sum_{1}^{Z}a_{i})}.

Proof.

The first part of the statement follows by
[
Lyl07
, Theorem 3.3]
.
To calculate the degree, notice that
\deg(\mathtt{T}^{\mu})=\sum_{1}^{Z}a_{i}
.
Hence

\deg(\mathtt{T}^{(1^{n})})-\deg(\mathtt{T}^{\mu})=-\sum_{1}^{Z}a_{i}.\qed

Example 4.39
.

Let
\mu=(7,3,2)
. If
p=2
, then
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{(12)})=1
.
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}
\vphantom{1}\smash{7}
\vphantom{1}\smash{8}
\vphantom{1}\smash{9}
\vphantom{1}\smash{10}
\vphantom{1}\smash{11}
\vphantom{1}\smash{12}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{6}
\vphantom{1}\smash{7}
\vphantom{1}\smash{8}
\vphantom{1}\smash{9}
\vphantom{1}\smash{10}
\vphantom{1}\smash{11}
\vphantom{1}\smash{12}

We can easily compute the graded dimension by calculating the degrees of
\mathtt{T}^{\mu}
and
\mathtt{T}^{\lambda}
:

\deg\mathtt{T}^{\lambda}-\deg\mathtt{T}^{\mu}=0-5=-5.

Hence
\dim_{q}\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{(1^{12})})=q^{-5}
.

Remark
.

If
\lambda=(n)
where depending on the parity
n=2d
or
2d+1
, then
\deg(\mathtt{T}^{\lambda})=d
.
Thus

\deg(\mathtt{T}^{(n)})-\deg(\mathtt{T}^{\mu})=d-\sum_{1}^{Z}a_{i}.

4.5.
Further questions remaining

Our strategy relied upon reducing more complex shapes (without complicated Garnir relations) into single rows or columns and then applying
4.19
.
In this paper, we gave a complete description of
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
where
\mu
can be visualised as a hook partition with a rectangle of width two in the middle.
The first row having even parity and no other rows having length more than two eliminated complicated Garnir relations.
We would like to emphasise that shapes of
\mu
with non-trivial Garnir relations seem to require an entirely different treatment as there are more relations to consider and the combinatorics governing the coefficients of the tableaux in the homomorphism space is different.
As we see in
4.7
, the
\Delta
coefficients are all products of positive integers.
One of the main reasons that prevented us to further continue our research into other shapes of
\mu
was the fact that even traditionally “well-behaving” shapes, such as two-part partitions, give rise to homomorphisms which have large prime numbers as coefficients.

Nevertheless, using results from
[
DG15
]
, we can present a simple proof for the case when
n
is odd and
\mu
is a two-row partition, however we note that this method does not yield an explicit description of the homomorphism or the degree.

Lemma 4.40
.

Assume that
n
is odd and let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
and
\lambda=(a,1^{b})
be two partitions of
n
with
\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq 2
.
If
\Ch(\mathbb{F})=2
, then
\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
is one dimensional if and only if
\mu_{2}\leq a-1\leq\mu_{1}
if and only if
\mu_{2}\leq b+1\leq\mu_{1}
.

Proof.

We note that our Specht modules are dual to those defined by James, so that
(S^{\lambda})^{\circledast}
denotes the Specht modules indexed by
\lambda
as in
[
Jam78
]
.

Let
e=2
and
\Ch\mathbb{F}=p=2
, so
R^{\Lambda}_{n}\cong\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.
We write
\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
throughout the proof, but all necessary Hecke algebra analogues can be found in
[
DG20
]
, except for the Littlewood–Richardson rule.
Although it holds in the Grothendieck group, we cannot find a Hecke analogue of
[
JP79
, Thm. 5.3]
in the literature, however it is almost certainly true.

We want to understand

\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{n}}(S^{(c+d,c)},S^{(a,1^{b})})\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{n}}((S^{(b+1,1^{a-1})^{\circledast}},(S^{(2^{c},1^{d})})^{\circledast}).

We may assume that
(S^{(a,1^{b})})^{\circledast}
and
(S^{(2^{c},1^{d})})^{\circledast}
lie in the same block, as otherwise there exists no homomorphisms between them.
As
n
is odd, we have that

M(a,b)=(S^{(a,1^{b})})^{\circledast}\oplus(S^{(a+1,1^{b-1})})^{\circledast}

since
(S^{(a,1^{b})})^{\circledast}
and
(S^{(a+1,1^{b-1})})^{\circledast}
are in different blocks (see
[
DG20
, Prop. 7.1.1]
).

Set
\tau=(2^{c},1^{d})
.
It follows that
(S^{(a+1,1^{b-1})})^{\circledast}
and
(S^{\tau})^{\circledast}
also lie in different blocks and so

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{n}}(M(a,b),(S^{\tau})^{\circledast})

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{n}}((S^{(a,1^{b})})^{\circledast},(S^{\tau})^{\circledast})\oplus\underbrace{\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{n}}(S^{(a+1,1^{b-1})})^{\circledast},(S^{\tau})^{\circledast})}_{=0}

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{n}}((S^{(a,1^{b})})^{\circledast},(S^{\tau})^{\circledast})

We also have that

\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{n}}(M(a,b),(S^{\tau})^{\circledast})\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{F}(\mathfrak{S}_{a}\times\mathfrak{S}_{b})}(1_{\mathfrak{S}_{a}\times\mathfrak{S}_{b}},(S^{\tau})^{\circledast}\downarrow_{\mathfrak{S}_{a}\times\mathfrak{S}_{b}}).

By
[
DG15
, Lemma 1.3.9.(i)]
,
(S^{\tau})^{\circledast}\downarrow_{\mathfrak{S}_{a}\times\mathfrak{S}_{b}}
has a filtration with factors isomorphic to
(S^{\nu})^{\circledast}\otimes(S^{(\tau/\nu)})^{\circledast}
such that
\nu\subseteq\tau
.
Furthermore, each submodule
(S^{(\tau/\nu)})^{\circledast}
has a Specht filtration and these factors
(S^{\xi})^{\circledast}
are given by the Littlewood–Richardson rule
[
DG15
, Lemma 1.3.9.(ii)]
.
It follows that

\dim\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{n}}((S^{(a,1^{b})})^{\circledast},(S^{\tau})^{\circledast})\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\vdash a\\
\mu\vdash b\end{subarray}}\dim\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{a}\times\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{b}}(1_{\mathfrak{S}_{a}\times\mathfrak{S}_{b}},(S^{\nu})^{\circledast}\otimes(S^{\mu})^{\circledast}).

By Kunneth’s formula, we also have that

\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{a}\times\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{b}}(1_{\mathfrak{S}_{a}\times\mathfrak{S}_{b}},(S^{\nu})^{\circledast}\otimes(S^{\mu})^{\circledast})\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{a}}(1_{\mathfrak{S}_{a}},(S^{\nu})^{\circledast})\otimes\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{b}}(1_{\mathfrak{S}_{b}},(S^{\mu})^{\circledast})

Recall that
\nu_{1},\mu_{1}\leq 2
and so by James’s Theorem (
[
Jam78
, Theorem 24.4]
), we have that

\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{a}}(1_{\mathfrak{S}_{a}},(S^{\nu})^{\circledast})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{F}\qquad\qquad\text{if }\nu=(2)\text{ or }\nu=(1^{a}),\\
0\qquad\qquad\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

and similarly

\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{b}}(1_{\mathfrak{S}_{b}},(S^{\mu})^{\circledast})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{F}\qquad\qquad\text{if }\mu=(2)\text{ or }\mu=(1^{b}),\\
0\qquad\qquad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

First, assume that
\tau=(2^{c},1^{d})\neq(2,1^{b})
.
It is easy to see that if
a<c
or
a>c+d
, then either
\mu
or
\nu
cannot be as required by James’s Theorem and so

\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{a}}(1_{\mathfrak{S}_{a}},(S^{\nu})^{\circledast})\otimes\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{b}}(1_{\mathfrak{S}_{b}},(S^{\mu})^{\circledast})=0

for all
\mu,\nu
, which gives

\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{n}}((S^{(a,1^{b})})^{\circledast},(S^{\tau})^{\circledast})=0.

On the other hand, if
c\leq a\leq c+d
, then we see that
(S^{(2^{c},1^{d})})^{\circledast}\downarrow_{\mathfrak{S}_{a}\times\mathfrak{S}_{b}}
has
(S^{(1^{a})})^{\circledast}\otimes(S^{(1^{b})})^{\circledast}
as a submodule while the other factors are of the form
(S^{\nu})^{\circledast}\otimes(S^{\mu})^{\circledast}
and they do not satisfy the conditions in James’s Theorem and thus

\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{n}}((S^{(a,1^{b})})^{\circledast},(S^{\tau})^{\circledast})\cong\mathbb{F}.

Finally, if
\tau=(2,1^{b})
, then

\End_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{n}}((S^{\tau})^{\circledast})\cong\mathbb{F},

since it is a
2
-restricted partition.

Thus

\Hom_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{n}}((S^{(a,1^{b})})^{\circledast},(S^{(2^{c},1^{d})})^{\circledast})

is one dimensional if
c\leq a\leq c+d
and zero otherwise.
Hence by the first isomorphism in our proof, for
\lambda=(a,1^{b})
and
\mu=(c+d,c)
such that
a+b=n
is odd, we have that
\Hom(S^{\lambda},S^{\mu})
is one dimensional if
c\leq a-1\leq c+d
and
c\leq b+1\leq c+d
.
∎

Remark
.

We note that
4.40
considers ungraded homomorphisms.
The above method can also be applied to two-column partitions, however unlike our result (
4.33
), it does not give an explicit description of the homomorphism.
